You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

When a warrior wants to talk with another one about peace or war he uses a smartphone.  In one distinct  country warriors who spent all time in training kata not always have enough money. So if they call some number they want to know which operator serves  this number. 

Write a function which **accepts number and return name of operator or string "no info"**, if operator can't be defined. number always looks like 8yyyxxxxxxx, where yyy corresponds to operator.

Here is short list of operators:

* 039 xxx xx xx - Golden Telecom
* 050 xxx xx xx - MTS
* 063 xxx xx xx - Life:)
* 066 xxx xx xx - MTS
* 067 xxx xx xx - Kyivstar
* 068 xxx xx xx - Beeline
* 093 xxx xx xx - Life:)
* 095 xxx xx xx - MTS
* 096 xxx xx xx - Kyivstar
* 097 xxx xx xx - Kyivstar
* 098 xxx xx xx - Kyivstar
* 099 xxx xx xx - MTS Test [Just return "MTS"]

Write your solution by modifying this code:

```python
def detect_operator(num):
	
```

Your solution should implemented in the function "detect_operator". The inputs will be passed to it and it should return the correct solution.


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to identify which mobile operator serves a given phone number
2. The phone number format is always `8yyyxxxxxxx` where `yyy` is the operator code
3. We have a list of operators with their codes and names
4. We need to return the operator name or "no info" if the operator isn't found

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the pattern
- The phone number starts with "8"
- The next 3 digits (yyy) represent the operator code
- We need to extract these 3 digits from the input number

### Step 2: Map operator codes to names
From the given list:
- 039 → Golden Telecom
- 050 → MTS
- 063 → Life:)
- 066 → MTS
- 067 → Kyivstar
- 068 → Beeline
- 093 → Life:)
- 095 → MTS
- 096 → Kyivstar
- 097 → Kyivstar
- 098 → Kyivstar
- 099 → MTS (Test case)

### Step 3: Extract the operator code
- The input number starts with "8"
- We need to take the next 3 digits after "8"
- For example, "80631234567" → the operator code is "063"

### Step 4: Implement the solution
- Extract the 3-digit operator code from the input
- Check which operator it matches
- Return the corresponding name, or "no info" if not found

## Code Implementation

```python
def detect_operator(num):
    # Extract the operator code (the 3 digits after "8")
    # Remove the "8" at the beginning and take the first 3 digits
    operator_code = num[1:4]
    
    # Map operator codes to names
    operator_map = {
        '039': 'Golden Telecom',
        '050': 'MTS',
        '063': 'Life:',
        '066': 'MTS',
        '067': 'Kyivstar',
        '068': 'Beeline',
        '093': 'Life:',
        '095': 'MTS',
        '096': 'Kyivstar',
        '097': 'Kyivstar',
        '098': 'Kyivstar',
        '099': 'MTS'
    }
    
    # Return the operator name if found, otherwise "no info"
    return operator_map.get(operator_code, "no info")
```

The solution works by:
1. Extracting the 3-digit operator code from the input number (by slicing from index 1 to 4)
2. Using a dictionary to map each operator code to its name
3. Using the `.get()` method to safely retrieve the value, returning "no info" if the code is not in the map

This handles all the given operator codes and will return the correct operator name or "no info" if the code doesn't match any known operator.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to identify which operator serves a given phone number
2. The phone number format is `8yyyxxxxxxx` where `yyy` is the operator code
3. We have a list of operators with their corresponding codes
4. If no operator matches, return "no info"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the pattern
- The phone number starts with "8"
- The next 3 digits (yyy) represent the operator code
- We need to extract these 3 digits from the input

### Step 2: Identify the mapping
From the given information:
- 039 → Golden Telecom
- 050 → MTS
- 063 → Life:)
- 066 → MTS
- 067 → Kyivstar
- 068 → Beeline
- 093 → Life:)
- 095 → MTS
- 096 → Kyivstar
- 097 → Kyivstar
- 098 → Kyivstar
- 099 → MTS (Test - return "MTS")

### Step 3: Algorithm
1. Input the phone number string
2. Extract the 3 digits after "8" (positions 1-3)
3. Check which operator corresponds to those digits
4. Return the operator name or "no info" if not found

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def detect_operator(num):
    # Extract the 3-digit operator code (positions 1-3, since string starts with "8")
    operator_code = num[1:4]
    
    # Create a mapping from operator code to operator name
    operator_map = {
        "039": "Golden Telecom",
        "050": "MTS",
        "063": "Life:",
        "066": "MTS",
        "067": "Kyivstar",
        "068": "Beeline",
        "093": "Life:",
        "095": "MTS",
        "096": "Kyivstar",
        "097": "Kyivstar",
        "098": "Kyivstar",
        "099": "MTS"
    }
    
    # Return the corresponding operator name or "no info" if not found
    return operator_map.get(operator_code, "no info")
```

## Verification
Let's check with a few examples:
- "80391234567" → operator_code = "039" → "Golden Telecom" ✓
- "80501234567" → operator_code = "050" → "MTS" ✓  
- "80631234567" → operator_code = "063" → "Life:" ✓
- "80991234567" → operator_code = "099" → "MTS" ✓
- "81231234567" → operator_code = "123" → "no info" ✓

The solution correctly handles all cases including the special case for 099 which returns "MTS".

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a phone number format: `8yyyxxxxxxx` where `yyy` is the operator code
2. We need to identify which operator serves this number based on the 3-digit code
3. If no match is found, return "no info"
4. The operators are given with specific codes:
   - 039: Golden Telecom
   - 050: MTS
   - 063: Life:)
   - 066: MTS
   - 067: Kyivstar
   - 068: Beeline
   - 093: Life:)
   - 095: MTS
   - 096: Kyivstar
   - 097: Kyivstar
   - 098: Kyivstar
   - 099: MTS (special case)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Extract the operator code
- The input number is in format `8yyyxxxxxxx`
- We need to extract the 3 digits after "8"
- This can be done by taking the substring from index 1 to 4 (0-indexed)

### Step 2: Map operator codes to operator names
- Create a dictionary mapping the 3-digit codes to operator names
- Include all the given operators

### Step 3: Return the appropriate result
- If the code exists in our dictionary, return the corresponding operator name
- If not found, return "no info"

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def detect_operator(num):
    # Extract the 3-digit operator code (positions 1 to 3 in 0-indexed)
    # The format is 8yyyxxxxxxx, so we take substring from index 1 to 4
    operator_code = num[1:4]
    
    # Create a mapping of operator codes to operator names
    operator_map = {
        "039": "Golden Telecom",
        "050": "MTS",
        "063": "Life:",
        "066": "MTS",
        "067": "Kyivstar",
        "068": "Beeline",
        "093": "Life:",
        "095": "MTS",
        "096": "Kyivstar",
        "097": "Kyivstar",
        "098": "Kyivstar",
        "099": "MTS"
    }
    
    # Return the operator name if found, otherwise "no info"
    return operator_map.get(operator_code, "no info")
```

The solution works by:
1. Taking the input number and extracting the 3-digit operator code (from index 1 to 4)
2. Using a dictionary to map each operator code to its name
3. Using the `.get()` method to safely retrieve the value, returning "no info" if the code isn't found in our mapping

This handles all the given cases and will correctly identify the operator or return "no info" for unknown codes.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to identify which telecom operator serves a given phone number
2. The phone number format is `8yyyxxxxxxx` where `yyy` is the operator code
3. We need to map specific operator codes to their names
4. If no operator is found, return "no info"

## Step-by-step Analysis

1. **Extract the operator code**: The number starts with "8" followed by 3 digits (yyy), so we need to extract the 3 digits after "8"
2. **Map operator codes to names**: Based on the given list, we have:
   - 039 → Golden Telecom
   - 050 → MTS
   - 063 → Life:)
   - 066 → MTS
   - 067 → Kyivstar
   - 068 → Beeline
   - 093 → Life:)
   - 095 → MTS
   - 096 → Kyivstar
   - 097 → Kyivstar
   - 098 → Kyivstar
   - 099 → MTS (test case)

3. **Implementation approach**: 
   - Extract the 3-digit operator code from the input number
   - Use a dictionary to map operator codes to their names
   - Return the corresponding name or "no info" if not found

## Edge Cases to Consider

- The input will always be in format `8yyyxxxxxxx`
- We need to ensure we extract only the 3 digits after "8"
- All provided codes are 3 digits long

## Solution

```python
def detect_operator(num):
    # Extract the 3-digit operator code (positions 1-3 after '8')
    operator_code = num[1:4]
    
    # Create mapping from operator codes to operator names
    operator_map = {
        "039": "Golden Telecom",
        "050": "MTS",
        "063": "Life:",
        "066": "MTS",
        "067": "Kyivstar",
        "068": "Beeline",
        "093": "Life:",
        "095": "MTS",
        "096": "Kyivstar",
        "097": "Kyivstar",
        "098": "Kyivstar",
        "099": "MTS"
    }
    
    # Return the operator name if found, otherwise "no info"
    return operator_map.get(operator_code, "no info")
```

The solution works by:
1. Taking the input number string
2. Extracting the 3-digit operator code starting from index 1 (after the leading '8')
3. Using a dictionary to quickly look up the operator name
4. Using the `.get()` method with a default value of "no info" to handle missing keys

This approach is efficient with O(1) time complexity for the lookup operation.

2. The phone number format is `8yyyxxxxxxx` where `yyyy` is the operator code
3. We have a list of operators with their corresponding codes
4. We need to return the operator name or "no info" if the operator is not found

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the pattern
- The phone number starts with "8"
- The next 3 digits (after "8") are the operator code
- We need to extract these 3 digits and match them to the known operators

### Step 2: Identify the operator codes and their names
From the problem statement:
- 039 → Golden Telecom
- 050 → MTS
- 063 → Life:)
- 066 → MTS
- 067 → Kyivstar
- 068 → Beeline
- 093 → Life:)
- 095 → MTS
- 096 → Kyivstar
- 097 → Kyivstar
- 098 → Kyivstar
- 099 → MTS (special case)

### Step 3: Algorithm
1. Extract the 3-digit operator code from the input number (positions 1-3 after "8")
2. Create a mapping from operator codes to operator names
3. Return the corresponding operator name, or "no info" if not found

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def detect_operator(num):
    # Extract the 3-digit operator code (positions 1 to 3 after "8")
    # The number format is 8yyyxxxxxxx, so we take digits[1:4]
    operator_code = num[1:4]
    
    # Create a dictionary mapping operator codes to operator names
    operator_map = {
        "039": "Golden Telecom",
        "050": "MTS",
        "063": "Life:",
        "066": "MTS",
        "067": "Kyivstar",
        "068": "Beeline",
        "093": "Life:",
        "095": "MTS",
        "096": "Kyivstar",
        "097": "Kyivstar",
        "098": "Kyivstar",
        "099": "MTS"
    }
    
    # Return the operator name if found, otherwise "no info"
    return operator_map.get(operator_code, "no info")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Everybody in Russia uses Gregorian calendar. In this calendar there are 31 days in January, 28 or 29 days in February (depending on whether the year is leap or not), 31 days in March, 30 days in April, 31 days in May, 30 in June, 31 in July, 31 in August, 30 in September, 31 in October, 30 in November, 31 in December.

A year is leap in one of two cases: either its number is divisible by 4, but not divisible by 100, or is divisible by 400. For example, the following years are leap: 2000, 2004, but years 1900 and 2018 are not leap.

In this problem you are given n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n}, and you have to check if these integers could be durations in days of n consecutive months, according to Gregorian calendar. Note that these months could belong to several consecutive years. In other words, check if there is a month in some year, such that its duration is a_1 days, duration of the next month is a_2 days, and so on.


-----Input-----

The first line contains single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) — the number of integers.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (28 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 31) — the numbers you are to check.


-----Output-----

If there are several consecutive months that fit the sequence, print "YES" (without quotes). Otherwise, print "NO" (without quotes).

You can print each letter in arbitrary case (small or large).


-----Examples-----
Input
4
31 31 30 31

Output
Yes


Input
2
30 30

Output
No


Input
5
29 31 30 31 30

Output
Yes


Input
3
31 28 30

Output
No


Input
3
31 31 28

Output
Yes




-----Note-----

In the first example the integers can denote months July, August, September and October.

In the second example the answer is no, because there are no two consecutive months each having 30 days.

In the third example the months are: February (leap year) — March — April – May — June.

In the fourth example the number of days in the second month is 28, so this is February. March follows February and has 31 days, but not 30, so the answer is NO.

In the fifth example the months are: December — January — February (non-leap year).

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to check if a given sequence of days can represent durations of n consecutive months according to the Gregorian calendar.
2. The months have fixed lengths: Jan(31), Feb(28/29), Mar(31), Apr(30), May(31), Jun(30), Jul(31), Aug(31), Sep(30), Oct(31), Nov(30), Dec(31)
3. February has 28 days in normal years and 29 days in leap years
4. A year is leap if divisible by 4 but not by 100, OR divisible by 400
5. The months can span multiple years, so we need to determine if there's a starting point where the sequence matches consecutive months

## Step-by-Step Solution

- January: 31

- March: 31
- April: 30
- May: 31
- June: 30
- July: 31
- August: 31
- September: 30
- October: 31
- November: 30
- December: 31

- When we go from December to January, we cross years
- When we go from February to March, we need to consider leap year or not

### Step 6: Implementation

```python
def is_leap(year):
    return (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0)

# Month lengths in a non-leap year
month_lengths = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

def get_month_length(month_idx, is_leap):
    if month_idx == 1:  # February
        return 29 if is_leap else 28
    else:
        return month_lengths[month_idx]

# Check if sequence works starting from month_idx with a given leap status
def check_sequence(start_month, sequence, leap_status):
    # We need to determine if the sequence matches consecutive months
    # The sequence can wrap around from December to January
    n = len(sequence)
    
    # For each month in the sequence
    for i in range(n):
        month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
        expected_length = get_month_length(month_idx, leap_status)
        if expected_length != sequence[i]:
            return False
    return True

# Main solution

# This makes it complex.

Let me reconsider with simpler logic:

The sequence represents n consecutive months. We can start at any month, and we can choose which year we start in (and thus the leap status).

For any sequence that doesn't contain February, we just need to verify that the month lengths match the expected pattern.

For sequences that contain February, we can try both leap and non-leap for each occurrence of February in the sequence, but since the years are consecutive, we need to determine if we can assign leap/non-leap to each year to make the sequence valid.

Actually, the constraint is that each year has one leap status. For a sequence of n months, there are at most floor(n/12) + 1 years. 

But n <= 24, so at most 3 years.

Since the leap status of a year is determined by its year number (divisible by 4, etc.), we cannot arbitrarily assign leap status - we have to follow the Gregorian rules.

This means we cannot freely choose leap status for each year. The leap status of year Y depends on Y%4, Y%100, Y%400.

So for a given starting month and a given year, we can determine the leap status.

Therefore, for each possible starting month (0 to 11), and for each possible starting year, we can check if the sequence works.

However, the starting year can be any year, so we have infinite possibilities. But since n is small (<=24), the leap status of the years in the sequence only depends on the year number modulo 400 (since leap year cycles every 400 years).

Therefore, for a given starting month and starting year, we can determine the leap status of each month in the sequence.

But we don't know the starting year. However, we can check if there exists a year Y such that when we start at month M of year Y, the sequence matches.

Since the leap status of years repeats every 400 years, we only need to check Y from 0 to 399.

But 400 * 12 = 4800 possible month combinations, and n <= 24, so checking 400 starting years for each of 12 starting months would be 12 * 400 = 4800, which is acceptable.

But we can do better - we only need to consider the leap status of the years that appear in the sequence.

The sequence has n months, which spans at most ceil(n/12) years. Since n <= 24, this is at most 3 years.

For each possible starting month M (0-11), and for each possible starting year Y mod 400, we can determine the leap status for each month in the sequence and check if it matches.

But there are only 12 * 400 = 4800 cases, which is acceptable for the constraints.

Alternatively, we can be smarter: since the leap status of years repeats every 400 years, and since we only need to check for the given month lengths, we can try a few year values.

But to be safe and simple, I'll try all possible starting months and all possible starting years from 0 to 399.

Wait, but we don't need to check every year from 0 to 399. The leap status of years depends on their number, and we can be more efficient.

Actually, I can realize that for a given starting month and a given position in the sequence, the leap status is determined by the year number. Since the sequence is of length n, and months are consecutive, the years are consecutive.

For a starting year Y, the year containing month i is Y + floor((start_month + i) / 12).

But since we only care about whether February is 28 or 29, and February is at month index 1, we need to check for each occurrence of month 1 in the sequence.

For a given starting month M and starting year Y, the sequence goes from (Y, M) to (Y + floor((M + n - 1)/12), (M + n - 1) % 12).

The years that appear are Y, Y+1, ..., Y + k where k = floor((M + n - 1)/12) - floor((M)/12).

For each month in the sequence, if the month index is 1 (February), we need to check if the year is leap.

So for each possible starting month M (0 to 11), and for each possible starting year Y in a reasonable range (we can try Y from 0 to 100, since leap cycles every 400 years), we can check if the sequence works.

But to be safe, I'll try Y from 0 to 399, since 400 is small.

However, there's an even simpler insight: we don't actually need to try all years. We only care about the leap status of years that contain February in the sequence. And since the sequence has at most 3 years, we can try each possible leap status combination for those years.

But the year numbers are determined by the starting year plus the number of years passed.

Actually, let me implement a simpler version: just try all starting months (0 to 11), and for each, try both leap and non-leap for February (if February appears in the sequence). But this is flawed because if the sequence spans multiple years, and February appears in year Y and year Y+1, we need to consider both leap and non-leap for each year.

Since the sequence length is small (n <= 24), at most 3 years, and each year has a fixed leap status based on its year number, we can't arbitrarily assign leap status.

Therefore, I need to try different starting years.

Given the constraints (n <= 24), and that leap years repeat every 400 years, I can try starting years from 0 to 399 for each starting month.

But 12 * 400 = 4800 iterations, which is fine.

Alternatively, I can optimize by noting that leap status only depends on the year mod 400, so I only need to try Y from 0 to 399.

Let me implement that.

Actually, let me use a different observation: since the sequence is consecutive months, the only variable is the leap status of the years that contain February. For a given starting month and sequence length, I can calculate which years contain February, and then for each such year, the leap status is determined by the year number.

But since I don't know the starting year, I can try all possible years from 0 to 399.

I'll implement the direct simulation.

```python
n = int(input())
sequence = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Month lengths in non-leap year
months = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Function to check if a sequence starting from month start_month in year start_year works
def check_sequence(start_month, start_year):
    # Simulate n consecutive months starting from (start_year, start_month)
    for i in range(n):
        month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
        year_offset = (start_month + i) // 12
        year = start_year + year_offset
        
        # Determine if this year is leap
        is_leap_year = (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0)
        
        # Get the length of the month
        if month_idx == 1:  # February
            month_length = 29 if is_leap_year else 28
        else:
            month_length = months[month_idx]
            
        if month_length != sequence[i]:
            return False
    return True

# Try all possible starting months (0 to 11) and all possible starting years (0 to 399)
# Since leap status repeats every 400 years, we only need to check 0 to 399
found = False
for start_month in range(12):
    for start_year in range(400):
        if check_sequence(start_month, start_year):
            found = True
            break
    if found:
        break

if found:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Wait, the example input 31 31 28: This should return "Yes" according to the note.

Let's verify: 31, 31, 28
- This could be December (31) -> January (31) -> February (28)
- In a non-leap year, December has 31 days, January has 31 days, February has 28 days.
- So starting at December (month 11) in a non-leap year should work.

In my code, for start_month = 11, start_year = 0:
- i=0: month_idx = 11 % 12 = 11, year_offset = 11 // 12 = 0, year = 0, not leap (0%4=0, 0%100=0, so not leap), month_length = months[11] = 31, sequence[0] = 31 ✓
- i=1: month_idx = (11+1) % 12 = 0, year_offset = (11+1) // 12 = 1, year = 1, not leap (1%4=1), month_length = months[0] = 31, sequence[1] = 31 ✓
- i=2: month_idx = (11+2) % 12 = 1, year_offset = (11+2) // 12 = 1, year = 1, not leap, month_length = 28, sequence[2] = 28 ✓

So it should work.

Another example: 30 30 - should return "No"
- 30, 30: consecutive months with 30 days each
- April (30) -> May (31) - doesn't match
- June (30) -> July (31) - doesn't match  
- September (30) -> October (31) - doesn't match
- November (30) -> December (31) - doesn't match
- The only consecutive months with 30 days would be April and May, but May has 31 days
- So no such sequence.

- February (29) -> March (31) -> April (30) -> May (31) -> June (30)
- This happens in a leap year: February has 29 days, then following months are as expected.

In my code: start_month = 1 (February), start_year = 0 (leap year)
- year 0 is leap: 0%4=0, 0%100=0, 0%400=0 -> not leap? 0%4=0, 0%100=0, so (0%4==0 and 0%100!=0) is false, and 0%400==0, so (0%400==0) is true, so leap? Wait: (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0)
- For year 0: year % 4 == 0, year % 100 == 0, so first condition is false, second condition year % 400 == 0 is true, so year 0 is leap. ✓

So it should work.

But wait: year 0 is divisible by 400, so it is leap. So it should work.

One more: 31 28 30 (should be "No")
- March (31) -> April (30) -> May (31) - should be 31,30,31
- The given is 31,28,30
- 28 is February, so this sequence would be... let's see: after March is April, which has 30 days, not 28.
- If it starts with February: February (28 or 29) -> March (31) -> April (30)
- So 28,31,30 or 29,31,30
- But we have 31,28,30 - so first is 31, second is 28
- The only month with 31 days before February is January or December or July, etc.
- If it starts with January: January (31) -> February (28) -> March (31) -> but we have 31,28,30 - so third month should be 30, but March is 31
- If it starts with December: December (31) -> January (31) -> February (28) -> months: 31,31,28
- We have 31,28,30 - so second is 28, third is 30
- After February is March, which has 31 days, not 30
- So no such sequence. ✓

Therefore, the code should work.

Let me write the final code with a small optimization: since the leap status repeats every 400 years, and 400 is reasonable, we can use 0 to 399.

One more thing: the input constraint says n >= 1 and <= 24, so the year offset is at most 2 years (since 24/12 = 2), so we only need to check years from start_year to start_year + 2.

But to be safe with the modulo, I'll keep the full 0 to 399.

Actually, since the leap status depends on the actual year number, and year numbers can be large, but they repeat every 400 years, we can reduce modulo 400.

So in the code, I can use start_year % 400.

But since start_year is from 0 to 399, it's already in range.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
sequence = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Month lengths in non-leap year
months = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Function to check if a sequence starting from month start_month in year start_year works
def check_sequence(start_month, start_year):
    for i in range(n):
        month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
        year_offset = (start_month + i) // 12
        year = start_year + year_offset
        
        # Determine if this year is leap
        is_leap_year = (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0)
        
        # Get the length of the month
        if month_idx == 1:  # February
            month_length = 29 if is_leap_year else 28
        else:
            month_length = months[month_idx]
            
        if month_length != sequence[i]:
            return False
    return True

# Try all possible starting months (0 to 11) and all possible starting years (0 to 399)
found = False
for start_month in range(12):
    for start_year in range(400):
        if check_sequence(start_month, start_year):
            found = True
            break
    if found:
        break

if found:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Everybody in Russia uses Gregorian calendar. In this calendar there are 31 days in January, 28 or 29 days in February (depending on whether the year is leap or not), 31 days in March, 30 days in April, 31 days in May, 30 in June, 31 in July, 31 in August, 30 in September, 31 in October, 30 in November, 31 in December.

A year is leap in one of two cases: either its number is divisible by 4, but not divisible by 100, or is divisible by 400. For example, the following years are leap: 2000, 2004, but years 1900 and 2018 are not leap.

In this problem you are given n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n}, and you have to check if these integers could be durations in days of n consecutive months, according to Gregorian calendar. Note that these months could belong to several consecutive years. In other words, check if there is a month in some year, such that its duration is a_1 days, duration of the next month is a_2 days, and so on.


-----Input-----

The first line contains single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) — the number of integers.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (28 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 31) — the numbers you are to check.


-----Output-----

If there are several consecutive months that fit the sequence, print "YES" (without quotes). Otherwise, print "NO" (without quotes).

You can print each letter in arbitrary case (small or large).


-----Examples-----
Input
4
31 31 30 31

Output
Yes


Input
2
30 30

Output
No


Input
5
29 31 30 31 30

Output
Yes


Input
3
31 28 30

Output
No


Input
3
31 31 28

Output
Yes




-----Note-----

In the first example the integers can denote months July, August, September and October.

In the second example the answer is no, because there are no two consecutive months each having 30 days.

In the third example the months are: February (leap year) — March — April – May — June.

In the fourth example the number of days in the second month is 28, so this is February. March follows February and has 31 days, but not 30, so the answer is NO.

In the fifth example the months are: December — January — February (non-leap year).

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to check if a given sequence of days can represent n consecutive months in the Gregorian calendar
2. Months have fixed lengths: Jan(31), Feb(28/29), Mar(31), Apr(30), May(31), Jun(30), Jul(31), Aug(31), Sep(30), Oct(31), Nov(30), Dec(31)
3. February is special - it has 28 days in common years and 29 days in leap years
4. A year is leap if:
   - Divisible by 4 but not by 100, OR
   - Divisible by 400
5. The months can span across years, so we need to check if there's a starting point where the sequence works

## Key Insights

1. Most months have fixed lengths (31 or 30 days)
2. Only February changes based on whether it's a leap year
3. We need to check all possible starting months and years
4. For each position in the sequence, we need to determine if that month can have the given number of days

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Define the standard month lengths for non-leap years
2. Create a mapping of month indices to their lengths (1-12)
3. For each possible starting month (1-12), we can simulate the sequence
4. For each position where we have February (month 2), we need to check if it's a leap year
5. The leap year status affects only February, so we can determine the leap status based on the month positions

## Algorithm

1. For each possible starting month (1 to 12):
   - Initialize a flag to track if we need a leap year
   - For each month in the sequence:
     - Determine which month it is (starting month + position - 1)
     - Handle month wrapping around years (mod 12)
     - Check if the current month position is February
     - If yes, determine if the year should be leap based on the month position
     - Verify the given day count matches the expected day count
     - If any check fails, this starting position doesn't work
2. If any starting position works, return "YES", otherwise "NO"

Let me trace through the examples:
- Example 1: 31,31,30,31 → July(31), Aug(31), Sep(30), Oct(31) → works
- Example 2: 30,30 → no two consecutive months both have 30 days (Apr, Jun, Sep, Nov) → fails
- Example 3: 29,31,30,31,30 → Feb(leap), Mar(31), Apr(30), May(31), Jun(30) → works  
- Example 4: 31,28,30 → Jan(31), Feb(28), Mar(31) but we need Mar to have 30 → fails
- Example 5: 31,31,28 → Dec(31), Jan(31), Feb(28) → works (Feb in non-leap year)

```python
def is_leap(year):
    return (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0)

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    days = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Standard month lengths for non-leap years
    # Index 0 = Jan, 1 = Feb, 2 = Mar, ..., 11 = Dec
    month_lengths = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
    
    # Try each possible starting month (0 to 11)
    for start_month in range(12):
        # We'll simulate the sequence starting from this month
        valid = True
        
        # We need to determine the year for each month
        # The year changes every 12 months
        # For each position, we can determine which month it is
        for i in range(n):
            # Current month index (0-11)
            month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
            
            # Check if this is February
            if month_idx == 1:  # February
                # We need to determine if it's a leap year
                # The year depends on the starting year
                # Since we don't know the exact year, we need to check if both leap and non-leap work
                # But actually, we can just check if the given day count is 28 or 29
                # If it's 28, it's non-leap year
                # If it's 29, it's leap year
                if days[i] == 29:
                    # For February to have 29 days, it must be a leap year
                    # We can't determine the exact year, so we just accept 29 for February
                    pass
                elif days[i] == 28:
                    # For February to have 28 days, it's non-leap year
                    pass
                else:
                    valid = False
                    break
            else:
                # For non-February months, check if the day count matches
                if days[i] != month_lengths[month_idx]:
                    valid = False
                    break
        
        if valid:
            return "YES"
    
    return "NO"

print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to reconsider. The issue is that for February, we don't know if it's leap or not. So when we see 28 days, it could be in a non-leap year, and when we see 29 days, it must be in a leap year.

But we can't have a contradiction. The problem is that we need to ensure the leap year status is consistent. Since months wrap around years, we need to handle the leap year consistency.

Actually, for each starting month, we can determine the year pattern. However, since we're just checking existence, and years can be arbitrary, we can handle February independently.

For each month in the sequence:
- If it's February (index 1), the day count must be either 28 or 29
- If it's not February, the day count must match the standard month length

But the key insight is that we don't need to worry about leap year consistency across months because the sequence can start in any year, and we can choose the year to make February have 28 or 29 days as needed.

For any given month position, as long as the day count is valid for that month type (31, 30, or 28/29 for February), it's possible.

So the algorithm is:
1. For each starting month (0-11):
   - For each position i in sequence:
     - Calculate target month = (start_month + i) % 12
     - If target month is February (1), check that days[i] is 28 or 29
     - If target month is not February, check that days[i] equals the standard length
   - If all checks pass, return "YES"
2. If no starting month works, return "NO"

```python
n = int(input())
days = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Standard month lengths for non-leap years
month_lengths = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Try each possible starting month (0 to 11)
for start_month in range(12):
    valid = True
    
    for i in range(n):
        # Current month index (0-11)
        month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
        
        if month_idx == 1:  # February
            # February can have 28 or 29 days
            if days[i] != 28 and days[i] != 29:
                valid = False
                break
        else:
            # For other months, check the standard length
            if days[i] != month_lengths[month_idx]:
                valid = False
                break
    
    if valid:
        print("YES")
        exit()

print("NO")

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to check if a given sequence of days can represent n consecutive months in the Gregorian calendar.
2. The days in each month are constrained by the calendar rules:
   - January, March, May, July, August, October, December: 31 days
   - April, June, September, November: 30 days
   - February: 28 days (29 in leap years)
3. We can span across years, so we need to handle leap years when February appears.
4. We need to check all possible starting months and years to see if the sequence matches.

## Key Insights

1. For each month in the sequence, we know what day count it should have based on the month name.
2. February can have 28 or 29 days depending on whether it's a leap year.
3. A leap year occurs when:
   - The year is divisible by 4, but not by 100, OR
   - The year is divisible by 400
4. We need to check if there exists a starting month (in some year) such that the sequence matches.

## Strategy

1. First, create a mapping of month names to their day counts:
   - January: 31, February: 28/29, March: 31, April: 30, May: 31, June: 30, July: 31, August: 31, September: 30, October: 31, November: 30, December: 31

2. For each possible starting month (1-12), and for each possible year (since we can span years), check if the sequence matches the expected day counts.

3. Since we only need to check if ANY sequence works, we can:
   - Try each starting month (1-12)
   - For each starting month, determine if the sequence can be satisfied by checking the constraints
   - Handle February specially since it depends on leap years

4. The key insight is that only February needs to be checked for leap year status. For any given position of February, we can determine if it's a leap year based on the year constraints.

5. We can iterate through all possible years, but since the pattern repeats every 400 years, we only need to check a few years. However, since n ≤ 24, we can just check the first few possible years or use a smarter approach.

## More Efficient Approach

Since the calendar pattern repeats every 400 years, we can check a limited range. But actually, we just need to determine if there exists some year where the sequence works.

For each possible starting position in the year cycle (1-12), we can:
- Determine the day count for each month in the sequence
- For February positions, we need to check if the leap year condition is satisfied

## Implementation Steps

1. Create a list of normal month days: [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
2. For each starting month (0 to 11), try to extend the sequence through months
3. When we encounter February (index 1), we need to check if it can be 29 days (leap year)
4. To determine if a February is leap, we need to know the year. Since months wrap around years, we can use modulo 400 to determine the leap year status.

Actually, since we don't know the starting year, we need to check if there exists some year where the pattern works.

For each possible starting month (0-11), we can simulate the months and for each February we encounter, we can check if it's possible for that February to have 28 or 29 days by checking the leap year condition.

But the year matters. We can define a function to check if a year is leap.

Let me implement this:

```python
def is_leap(year):
    return (year % 4 == 0 and year % 100 != 0) or (year % 400 == 0)

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    days = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Normal month lengths (non-leap year)
    normal_months = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
    
    # Try each possible starting month (0 to 11)
    for start_month in range(12):
        # We need to check if we can have a valid sequence starting at start_month
        # The sequence can span across years, so we need to determine leap years
        
        # For each position in the sequence, calculate what month it corresponds to
        # and check if the day count matches
        valid = True
        
        # We'll go through each position in the sequence
        for i in range(n):
            # Calculate the month index in the year cycle
            month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
            
            # The expected days for this month in a non-leap year
            expected_days = normal_months[month_idx]
            
            # For February (month index 1), we can have 28 or 29 days
            # If the given days is 29, then this February must be in a leap year
            # If the given days is 28, then it could be any year
            
            if month_idx == 1:  # February
                if days[i] == 29:
                    # This requires a leap year
                    # We need to determine if the year makes this leap
                    # The year depends on the offset from the start
                    # We can choose any year, so we just need to check if we can make it leap
                    # We can always construct a year where this February is in a leap year
                    # by choosing a year that is divisible by 4 but not by 100, or divisible by 400
                    # So this is always possible
                    pass
                elif days[i] == 28:
                    # This can happen in any year
                    pass
                else:
                    # days[i] > 31 or days[i] < 28 - impossible
                    valid = False
                    break
            else:
                # For other months, the day count must match exactly
                if days[i] != expected_days:
                    valid = False
                    break
                    
        if valid:
            print("YES")
            return
    
    print("NO")

solve()
```

- Months with 31 days: January, March, May, July, August, October, December
- Months with 30 days: April, June, September, November
- February: 28 or 29 days

So for any sequence to be valid, each element must be a valid month day count:
- Must be 28, 29, 30, or 31

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Everybody in Russia uses Gregorian calendar. In this calendar there are 31 days in January, 28 or 29 days in February (depending on whether the year is leap or not), 31 days in March, 30 days in April, 31 days in May, 30 in June, 31 in July, 31 in August, 30 in September, 31 in October, 30 in November, 31 in December.

A year is leap in one of two cases: either its number is divisible by 4, but not divisible by 100, or is divisible by 400. For example, the following years are leap: 2000, 2004, but years 1900 and 2018 are not leap.

In this problem you are given n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n}, and you have to check if these integers could be durations in days of n consecutive months, according to Gregorian calendar. Note that these months could belong to several consecutive years. In other words, check if there is a month in some year, such that its duration is a_1 days, duration of the next month is a_2 days, and so on.


-----Input-----

The first line contains single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) — the number of integers.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (28 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 31) — the numbers you are to check.


-----Output-----

If there are several consecutive months that fit the sequence, print "YES" (without quotes). Otherwise, print "NO" (without quotes).

You can print each letter in arbitrary case (small or large).


-----Examples-----
Input
4
31 31 30 31

Output
Yes


Input
2
30 30

Output
No


Input
5
29 31 30 31 30

Output
Yes


Input
3
31 28 30

Output
No


Input
3
31 31 28

Output
Yes




-----Note-----

In the first example the integers can denote months July, August, September and October.

In the second example the answer is no, because there are no two consecutive months each having 30 days.

In the third example the months are: February (leap year) — March — April – May — June.

In the fourth example the number of days in the second month is 28, so this is February. March follows February and has 31 days, but not 30, so the answer is NO.

In the fifth example the months are: December — January — February (non-leap year).

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Standard month lengths for non-leap year
month_lengths = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Try each possible starting month (0 to 11)
found = False
for start in range(12):
    valid = True
    for i in range(n):
        month_index = (start + i) % 12
        expected_days = month_lengths[month_index]
        
        # For February (index 1), we can have 28 or 29 days
        if month_index == 1:  # February
            if a[i] != 28 and a[i] != 29:
                valid = False
                break
        else:
            # For other months, days must exactly match standard length
            if a[i] != expected_days:
                valid = False
                break
    
    if valid:
        found = True
        break

if found:

    print("No")

In other words, check if there is a month in some year, such that its duration is a_1 days, duration of the next month is a_2 days, and so on.


-----Input-----

The first line contains single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) — the number of integers.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (28 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 31) — the numbers you are to check.


-----Output-----

If there are several consecutive months that fit the sequence, print "YES" (without quotes). Otherwise, print "NO" (without quotes).

You can print each letter in arbitrary case (small or large).


-----Examples-----
Input
4
31 31 30 31

Output
Yes


Input
2
30 30

Output
No


Input
5
29 31 30 31 30

Output
Yes


Input
3
31 28 30

Output
No


Input
3
31 31 28

Output
Yes




-----Note-----

In the first example the integers can denote months July, August, September and October.

In the second example the answer is no, because there are no two consecutive months each having 30 days.

In the third example the months are: February (leap year) — March — April – May — June.

In the fourth example the number of days in the second month is 28, so this is February. March follows February and has 31 days, but not 30, so the answer is NO.

In the fifth example the months are: December — January — February (non-leap year).

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to check if a given sequence of day counts could represent n consecutive months in the Gregorian calendar
2. The months must follow the actual calendar pattern (31, 28/29, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31)
3. February can have 28 or 29 days depending on whether it's a leap year
4. The months can span across multiple years

## Key Observations

1. The day counts must follow the monthly pattern in the Gregorian calendar
2. We need to consider February's special case - it can be 28 or 29 days
3. We need to find if there exists a starting month and year such that the sequence matches
4. The sequence must follow the pattern of consecutive months

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Identify valid month lengths**: 
   - January: 31
   - February: 28 or 29
   - March: 31
   - April: 30
   - May: 31
   - June: 30
   - July: 31
   - August: 31
   - September: 30
   - October: 31
   - November: 30
   - December: 31

2. **Check the sequence**:
   - For each possible starting position in the year, check if the sequence matches
   - For each month, check if the given day count is valid for that month
   - For February, check if the given day count is 28 or 29

3. **Handle the transition between years**:
   - We need to consider that after December comes January of the next year
   - The pattern repeats every year

4. **Possible starting months**:
   - Since we have at most 24 months, we need to check all possible starting positions
   - For each starting month, we simulate the sequence through consecutive months

## Algorithm

1. Define the valid day counts for each month
2. Create a mapping of month index to its valid day count (with February having two possibilities)
3. For each possible starting month (0 to 11), try to check if the sequence matches
4. For each starting month, simulate the next n months, considering leap years when needed
5. For February, if we have 29 days, it's a leap year; if we have 28 days, it's a non-leap year
6. When we go from December to January, we need to handle the year transition

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Define the number of days in each month (0-indexed)
# January: 31, February: 28 or 29, March: 31, April: 30, May: 31, June: 30, July: 31, August: 31, September: 30, October: 31, November: 30, December: 31
months_days = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Function to check if a given sequence can represent consecutive months
def can_form_sequence(sequence):
    # Try each starting month (0 to 11)
    for start_month in range(12):
        # Try to simulate the sequence starting from start_month
        year = 0  # We don't actually need to track year, just the month transitions
        valid = True
        
        for i in range(n):
            current_month = (start_month + i) % 12
            
            # Check if the given day count matches the expected month
            if current_month == 1:  # February
                # February can be 28 or 29
                if sequence[i] != 28 and sequence[i] != 29:
                    valid = False
                    break
            else:
                # For other months, the day count is fixed
                expected_days = months_days[current_month]
                if sequence[i] != expected_days:
                    valid = False
                    break
        
        if valid:
            return True
    
    return False

# However, there's a problem: we need to consider leap years
# The issue is that when we have February with 29 days, it's a leap year
# And this affects the following month (March), but March always has 31 days

# Let me reconsider: actually, in the sequence, if we have February with 29 days,
# it means we're in a leap year. But that doesn't affect other months directly.

# But we need to be careful - the calendar pattern is fixed, and only February varies.

# The main issue is that if we have 29 days in February, it's a leap year,
# but that doesn't change the day counts of other months.

# So my previous logic should work because:
# - For any month except February, the day count is fixed
# - For February, the day count can be 28 or 29

# However, the problem is that we need to check if a sequence of n consecutive months can exist,
# and the leap year status can be chosen to make the sequence valid.

# Let me refine: for each possible starting month, we want to check if the sequence matches,
# and for February positions, we only need to check if the value is 28 or 29.

# But there's one more constraint: when we have a sequence that includes multiple years,
# the leap year status might affect February in different years.

# Actually, looking at the examples:
# Example 3: 29 31 30 31 30 - February (leap) -> March -> April -> May -> June
# Example 4: 31 28 30 - December -> January -> February (non-leap) - this should be valid?

# Wait, example 4 says output is "No", but my analysis shows:
# December: 31, January: 31, February: 28 - but January should have 31 days, not 31?
# Let me check the example: Input 3: 31 28 30 -> Output: No

# Looking at example 4: 31 28 30 -> Output: No
# This sequence would be: December (31), January (28) - but January has 31 days, so this is impossible.

# Similarly, example 5: 31 31 28 -> Output: Yes
# This would be: December (31), January (31), February (28) - January has 31 days, so 31 is valid, February has 28 days which is valid.

# So the issue is that for months other than February, the day count is fixed.

# Algorithm:
# 1. For each possible starting month (0 to 11), try to form the sequence
# 2. For each position i, check if the value a[i] is valid for month (start_month + i) % 12
# 3. For months other than February, the value must exactly match the standard day count
# 4. For February, the value must be either 28 or 29

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Standard days for each month (0-indexed)
    standard_days = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
    
    # Try each possible starting month
    for start_month in range(12):
        valid = True
        
        for i in range(n):
            month_index = (start_month + i) % 12
            expected_days = standard_days[month_index]
            
            # For non-February months, the day count must match exactly
            if month_index != 1:  # February is index 1
                if a[i] != expected_days:
                    valid = False
                    break
            else:  # February
                # February can have 28 or 29 days
                if a[i] != 28 and a[i] != 29:
                    valid = False
                    break
        
        if valid:
            return "YES"
    
    return "NO"

print(solve())

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Everybody in Russia uses Gregorian calendar. In this calendar there are 31 days in January, 28 or 29 days in February (depending on whether the year is leap or not), 31 days in March, 30 days in April, 31 days in May, 30 in June, 31 in July, 31 in August, 30 in September, 31 in October, 30 in November, 31 in December.

A year is leap in one of two cases: either its number is divisible by 4, but not divisible by 100, or is divisible by 400. For example, the following years are leap: 2000, 2004, but years 1900 and 2018 are not leap.

In this problem you are given n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n}, and you have to check if these integers could be durations in days of n consecutive months, according to Gregorian calendar. Note that these months could belong to several consecutive years. In other words, check if there is a month in some year, such that its duration is a_1 days, duration of the next month is a_2 days, and so on.


-----Input-----

The first line contains single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) — the number of integers.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (28 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 31) — the numbers you are to check.


-----Output-----

If there are several consecutive months that fit the sequence, print "YES" (without quotes). Otherwise, print "NO" (without quotes).

You can print each letter in arbitrary case (small or large).


-----Examples-----
Input
4
31 31 30 31

Output
Yes

2
30 30

Output
No

5
29 31 30 31 30

Output
Yes

No

In the fifth example the months are: December — January — February (non-leap year).

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to check if a given sequence of day counts can represent n consecutive months in the Gregorian calendar
2. Months have fixed lengths: 31, 28/29 (leap), 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31
3. February can have 28 days (normal year) or 29 days (leap year)
4. The months can span across years, so we need to find if there's a starting point where the sequence matches consecutive months

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Define month lengths
- January: 31
- February: 28 (normal) or 29 (leap)
- March: 31
- April: 30
- May: 31
- June: 30
- July: 31
- August: 31
- September: 30
- October: 31
- November: 30
- December: 31

### Step 2: Identify valid month sequences
For any given month, we know what days it has:
- Months with fixed 31 days: Jan, Mar, May, Jul, Aug, Oct, Dec
- Month with fixed 30 days: Apr, Jun, Sep, Nov
- February: 28 or 29 days (we need to check both cases)

### Step 3: Algorithm approach
Since the sequence can span years, and only February can vary (28 or 29), we need to:
1. Check if the sequence matches a valid pattern of consecutive months
2. For each possible starting month, check if the sequence matches
3. For February, we need to try both 28 and 29 days

### Step 4: Implementation strategy
- Create a list of month lengths with February having both possibilities
- Try each month as a starting point
- For each starting point, check if the sequence matches consecutive months
- When we encounter February, we need to consider both 28 and 29 days

1. Create a list of valid month lengths: [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
2. For each possible starting month (12 possibilities), try both 28 and 29 for February

Wait, that's overcomplicating. Since we're going through consecutive months, if we hit February, we need to check if the given value matches 28 or 29.

But actually, we can just check: for each possible starting month, generate the sequence of month lengths for n consecutive months, considering that February can be 28 or 29. If the given sequence matches any such generated sequence, return "YES".

However, since months repeat every year, we just need to check if there exists a valid sequence of n consecutive months where the length of each month matches the given input.

### Step 5: Simplified approach
- Create a list of month lengths for a regular year: [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
- For each possible starting month (0 to 11), we can generate a sequence of n months
- For each position where the month is February (index 1), we have two options: 28 or 29
- So we need to check all possible combinations

Actually, let's be more precise. We can try each starting month index from 0 to 11, and for each position, if it's February (index 1), we try both 28 and 29. But we need to check all possible sequences.

However, the problem is simpler - we just need to check if there exists any sequence of n consecutive months (which could wrap around years) that matches the given sequence.

Since months repeat every 12 months, we can try starting at each of the 12 months, and for each, we check if the sequence matches consecutive months, with February having either 28 or 29 days.

So the algorithm:
1. Read n and the sequence a
2. Create month lengths: [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
3. For each starting month index from 0 to 11:
   - Create a sequence of n months starting from that month
   - For each position i in this sequence, if the month is February (index 1), we can have either 28 or 29 days
   - We need to check if there's a valid assignment of 28/29 to February such that the sequence matches
4. If any such valid sequence exists, return "YES", otherwise "NO"

But actually, we don't need to try all combinations - we only need to know if there's a valid assignment. For each starting point, we check if the given sequence matches the month lengths for that starting point, with February having either 28 or 29.

For any month except February, the length is fixed. For February, the given value must be either 28 or 29.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Month lengths in a regular year: Jan, Feb, Mar, Apr, May, Jun, Jul, Aug, Sep, Oct, Nov, Dec
# Fixed lengths: 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31
# February is index 1, so length 28 or 29
month_lengths = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Try each possible starting month (0 to 11)
found = False

for start_month in range(12):
    # Check if sequence matches n consecutive months starting from start_month
    valid = True
    for i in range(n):
        month_idx = (start_month + i) % 12
        expected_length = month_lengths[month_idx]
        
        # For February (index 1), we can have 28 or 29 days
        if month_idx == 1:  # February
            if a[i] != 28 and a[i] != 29:
                valid = False
                break
        else:
            # All other months have fixed lengths
            if a[i] != expected_length:
                valid = False
                break
    
    if valid:
        found = True
        break

if found:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

```python

a = list(map(int, input().split()))

month_lengths = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

    valid = True

        expected_length = month_lengths[month_idx]

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Everybody in Russia uses Gregorian calendar. In this calendar there are 31 days in January, 28 or 29 days in February (depending on whether the year is leap or not), 31 days in March, 30 days in April, 31 days in May, 30 in June, 31 in July, 31 in August, 30 in September, 31 in October, 30 in November, 31 in December.

A year is leap in one of two cases: either its number is divisible by 4, but not divisible by 100, or is divisible by 400. For example, the following years are leap: 2000, 2004, but years 1900 and 2018 are not leap.

In this problem you are given n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n}, and you have to check if these integers could be durations in days of n consecutive months, according to Gregorian calendar. Note that these months could belong to several consecutive years. In other words, check if there is a month in some year, such that its duration is a_1 days, duration of the next month is a_2 days, and so on.


-----Input-----

The first line contains single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 24) — the number of integers.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (28 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 31) — the numbers you are to check.


-----Output-----

If there are several consecutive months that fit the sequence, print "YES" (without quotes). Otherwise, print "NO" (without quotes).

You can print each letter in arbitrary case (small or large).

Yes

In the fourth example the number of days in the second month is 28, so this is February. March follows February and has 31 days, but not 30, so the answer is NO.

In the fifth example the months are: December — January — February (non-leap year).

4. The months can span across years, so we need to determine if there's a valid starting point and leap year pattern that makes the sequence work

## Key Insights

1. We need to find a valid starting month and determine if the leap year pattern makes the sequence valid
2. Since we only care about consecutive months, the pattern of leap years will only affect February
3. February can be 28 or 29 days depending on whether the year is leap
4. For a sequence to be valid, we need to check if each month duration matches the expected days in the Gregorian calendar
5. The only variable is whether February in the sequence is a leap year (29 days)

## Approach

3. For each case, verify that all other months have the correct number of days
4. The validity of a specific position depends on the leap year rules

# Standard days in each month (Jan to Dec)
standard_days = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

def is_valid_sequence(month_days, leap_year_at):
    """
    Check if month_days can be a valid sequence given that leap_year_at
    is the index (0-11) of the month that is a leap year (29 days)
    """
    # We need to check if the given sequence matches the calendar
    # For each month, check if it matches expected days
    for i in range(n):
        month_idx = i  # We're looking at the i-th month in the sequence
        # The actual month in the calendar (0-indexed)
        actual_month = month_idx % 12
        
        # Days in that month in a leap year
        if actual_month == 1 and leap_year_at == month_idx:
            expected_days = 29
        else:
            # Days in that month in a non-leap year
            expected_days = standard_days[actual_month]
        
        # Check if the expected days match the given sequence
        if month_days[i] != expected_days:
            return False
    
    return True

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Standard days for each month (0-indexed)
    standard_days = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

    for start_month in range(12):
        # Check if this starting month makes the sequence valid
        valid = True
        
        # For each position in the sequence
        for i in range(n):

                if a[i] == 29:

                    pass
                elif a[i] == 28:
                    # This is ok - it's a non-leap year
                    pass
                else:
                    # Invalid for February - only 28 or 29
                    valid = False
            else:
                # For all other months, the day count must exactly match
                if a[i] != expected_days:
                    valid = False
        
        if valid:
            print("YES")
            return
    
    print("NO")

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Standard days for each month (0-indexed)
standard_days = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Try every possible starting month (0 to 11)
for start_month in range(12):
    valid = True

    for i in range(n):

        calendar_month = (start_month + i) % 12
        
        # The expected days for this month
        expected_days = standard_days[calendar_month]
        
        # If this is February (month 1), we can have 28 or 29 days
        if calendar_month == 1:  # February
            # If the given day count is 29, it must be a leap year (valid)
            # If the given day count is 28, it must be a non-leap year (valid)
            if a[i] != 28 and a[i] != 29:
                valid = False
        else:
            # For all other months, the day count must exactly match
            if a[i] != expected_days:
                valid = False
    
    if valid:

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to check if a given sequence of days can represent consecutive months in the Gregorian calendar
2. Months have fixed day counts: January(31), February(28/29), March(31), April(30), May(31), June(30), July(31), August(31), September(30), October(31), November(30), December(31)
3. February has 29 days in leap years (divisible by 4 but not by 100, OR divisible by 400)
4. The months can span multiple years, so we only need to determine if the pattern works for some starting year
5. We need to check if there exists a valid starting month such that the sequence of month durations matches the calendar

## Approach

1. Create a list of standard month lengths in non-leap years
2. Create a list of month lengths in leap years (with February having 29 days)
3. For each possible starting month (1-12), try to see if the given sequence matches:
   - Start with that month and advance month by month
   - When we get to February, we need to check if it's a leap year or not
   - The leap year status affects only February
   - We can choose the leap year status to make the sequence valid
4. For a sequence to be valid, we need to check that each month duration matches the calendar, with the constraint that February can be 28 or 29 days depending on whether the year is leap

## Key Insight

Since months are consecutive and span multiple years, we only need to determine the leap year status for the years that contain February in our sequence. 

For each position in the sequence, if it corresponds to February (month 2), we have a choice: either leap year (29 days) or non-leap (28 days). We need to check if there exists a leap year setting such that all month lengths match.

## Solution Steps

1. Define the standard month lengths (non-leap year)
2. Define the leap year month lengths (with February = 29)
3. For each possible starting month (1-12), try to construct the sequence
4. For each month in the sequence, determine which month it is and check if the given day count matches
5. Special handling for February: we can choose whether it's leap or non-leap
6. If any starting month works, return "YES", otherwise "NO"

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    days = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Standard month lengths in non-leap year
    non_leap = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
    
    # Leap year month lengths (February has 29 days)
    leap = [31, 29, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
    
    # Check if a given sequence can be formed
    def can_form_sequence(start_month):
        # For each month in the sequence, we need to determine the actual length
        # based on which month it is and whether the year is leap
        # We only need to consider leap year status for months that contain February
        
        # We'll simulate month by month
        current_month = start_month
        valid = True
        
        for i in range(n):
            # Determine which month we're currently at
            month_index = (current_month - 1) % 12
            
            # Check if this month is February (index 1)
            if month_index == 1:  # February
                # We can choose leap or non-leap
                # If the given day count is 29, we need a leap year
                # If the given day count is 28, we need a non-leap year
                # Both are valid, so we just need to check it's either 28 or 29
                if days[i] != 28 and days[i] != 29:
                    valid = False
                    break
            else:
                # For other months, the day count is fixed
                if days[i] != non_leap[month_index] and days[i] != leap[month_index]:
                    valid = False
                    break
            
            # Move to next month
            current_month += 1
        
        return valid
    
    # Try all possible starting months (1 to 12)
    for start in range(1, 13):
        if can_form_sequence(start):
            print("YES")
            return
    
    print("NO")

solve()
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the logic. The issue with my current approach is that I'm not properly handling the fact that February only affects the year it's in, not every year. 

Actually, my current approach might work, but let me trace through the examples to verify:

Example 1: [31, 31, 30, 31] - July (31), Aug (31), Sep (30), Oct (31). This should work with any year since these are regular months.

Example 2: [30, 30] - Two consecutive months with 30 days. April and June both have 30 days, but they're not consecutive. May has 31 days, so April-June is not consecutive. Check: April (30), May (31), June (30) - can't have two 30-day months in a row. So this should be NO.

Example 3: [29, 31, 30, 31, 30] - February (29), March (31), April (30), May (31), June (30). This works when February is leap.

Example 4: [31, 28, 30] - January (31), February (28), March (30). February 28 works, but March is 30 days, but March has 31 days. So this should be NO.

Example 5: [31, 31, 28] - December (31), January (31), February (28). February 28 works in a non-leap year. This should be YES.

So my current algorithm should work. Let me verify the leap year handling:

For a month that is February (index 1), we check if the day count is 28 or 29. Since we can choose the leap year status, both values are valid.

For other months, the day count must match exactly. In a non-leap year, each month has a fixed length, and in a leap year only February changes.

But we don't have to use both leap and non-leap year patterns - we just need to see if there exists some configuration that works.

Actually, my algorithm is flawed. I'm assuming that for non-February months, both leap and non-leap years give the same month length, which is not true. Let me correct this.

For any month that is not February, the month length is fixed regardless of leap year status. For example, January is always 31 days, March is always 31 days, etc.

So my algorithm is actually correct. The only special case is February, where we can choose 28 or 29 days.

```python
n = int(input())
days = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Standard month lengths in non-leap year
non_leap = [31, 28, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]
# Leap year month lengths (with February having 29 days)
leap = [31, 29, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31, 31, 30, 31, 30, 31]

# Check if a given sequence can be formed starting from a specific month
def can_form_sequence(start_month):
    current_month = start_month
    valid = True
    
    for i in range(n):
        month_index = (current_month - 1) % 12
        
        # Check if this is February (index 1)
        if month_index == 1:  # February
            # February can be 28 or 29 days
            if days[i] != 28 and days[i] != 29:
                valid = False
                break
        else:
            # For other months, the day count must match the standard length
            # The length is the same in both leap and non-leap years
            if days[i] != non_leap[month_index]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        current_month += 1
    
    return valid

# Try all possible starting months (1 to 12)
found = False
for start in range(1, 13):
    if can_form_sequence(start):
        found = True
        break

if found:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an integer sequence A of length N whose values are unknown.
Given is an integer sequence B of length N-1 which is known to satisfy the following:
 B_i \geq \max(A_i, A_{i+1})

-----Constraints-----

 - 2 \leq N \leq 100
 - 0 \leq B_i \leq 10^5

N

-----Output-----
Print the maximum possible sum of the elements of A.

-----Sample Input-----
3
2 5

-----Sample Output-----
9

A can be, for example, ( 2 , 1 , 5 ), ( -1 , -2 , -3 ), or ( 2 , 2 , 5 ). Among those candidates, A = ( 2 , 2 , 5 ) has the maximum possible sum.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

The constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) means that for each position i (from 1 to N-1):
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

Wait, no. The min terms are for positions 2 to N-1, which corresponds to i from 1 to N-2.

Actually, the sum is:
- A_1 = B[0]
- A_2 = min(B[0], B[1])
- A_3 = min(B[1], B[2])
- ...
- A_{N-1} = min(B[N-3], B[N-2])
- A_N = B[N-2]

So sum = B[0] + Σ_{j=0}^{N-3} min(B[j], B[j+1]) + B[N-2]

For N=3: sum = B[0] + min(B[0], B[1]) + B[1] = 2 + min(2,5) + 5 = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

This works!

So algorithm:
1. Read N
2. Read B of length N-1
3. Sum = B[0] + B[N-2] + sum of min(B[i], B[i+1]) for i from 0 to N-3

Wait, the middle terms are min(B[i], B[i+1]) for i from 0 to N-3?
- For N=3: i from 0 to 0 (since N-3 = 0), so min(B[0], B[1]) ✓

Yes.

So final code:

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

# If N == 2, then B has 1 element
# Sum = A1 + A2 = B[0] + B[0] = 2*B[0]
# General formula: sum = B[0] + sum_{i=0}^{N-3} min(B[i], B[i+1]) + B[N-2]

total_sum = B[0] + B[N-2]  # A[0] and A[N-1]

# Add min(B[i], B[i+1]) for i from 0 to N-3 (which covers positions 2 to N-1 in A)
for i in range(N-2):
    total_sum += min(B[i], B[i+1])

print(total_sum)
```
