I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

The constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) means that for each i, both A_i and A_{i+1} must be ≤ B_i.

## Key Insight

To maximize the sum of A, we want each element A_i to be as large as possible while satisfying all constraints.

For each position i:
- A_i must be ≤ B_{i-1} (from the constraint involving B_{i-1})
- A_i must be ≤ B_i (from the constraint involving B_i)

Wait, let me reconsider the indexing. The constraint says B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}), which means:
- For position i: A_i ≤ B_i and A_{i+1} ≤ B_i
- For position i+1: A_{i+1} ≤ B_{i+1} and A_{i+2} ≤ B_{i+1}

So for each position j in A:
- A_j ≤ B_{j-1} (from constraint B_{j-1} ≥ max(A_{j-1}, A_j))
- A_j ≤ B_j (from constraint B_j ≥ max(A_j, A_{j+1}))

Actually, let me trace through the sample:
- N = 3, B = [2, 5]
- Constraint 1: B_1 = 2 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) → A_1 ≤ 2 and A_2 ≤ 2
- Constraint 2: B_2 = 5 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) → A_2 ≤ 5 and A_3 ≤ 5

To maximize sum A_1 + A_2 + A_3:
- A_1 ≤ 2 (from constraint 1)
- A_2 ≤ 2 (from constraint 1) and A_2 ≤ 5 (from constraint 2) → so A_2 ≤ 2
- A_3 ≤ 5 (from constraint 2)

Maximum values: A_1 = 2, A_2 = 2, A_3 = 5
Sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

## Pattern Recognition

For position i (1-indexed):
- A_i is constrained by B_{i-1} (when i > 1) and by B_i (when i < N)
- For A_1: only constraint is from B_1 → A_1 ≤ B_1
- For A_N: only constraint is from B_{N-1} → A_N ≤ B_{N-1}
- For 1 < i < N: A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

Wait, let me verify with position i:
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from constraint B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i))
- A_i ≤ B_i (from constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

So A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 1 < i < N.

But for the boundaries:
- A_1 ≤ B_1 (only constraint from B_1)
- A_N ≤ B_{N-1} (only constraint from B_{N-1})

Actually, for A_1: it's only constrained by B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2), so A_1 ≤ B_1
For A_N: it's only constrained by B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N), so A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

For A_i where 1 < i < N: constrained by both B_{i-1} and B_i, so A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

Therefore, the maximum sum is:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1
- A_N = B_{N-1}

Let me verify with the sample:
- N = 3, B = [2, 5]
- A_1 = B_1 = 2
- A_2 = min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2  
- A_3 = B_2 = 5
- Sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

## Algorithm

1. Read N and sequence B
2. Initialize sum = 0
3. For position 1: add B[0]
4. For positions 2 to N-1: add min(B[i-2], B[i-1]) 
5. For position N: add B[N-2]
6. Return sum

Wait, let me reindex properly. B has indices 0 to N-2.

For position i (0-indexed):
- A_0 ≤ B_0 (from constraint B_0 ≥ max(A_0, A_1))
- A_1 ≤ min(B_0, B_1) (from constraints B_0 ≥ max(A_0, A_1) and B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
- A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2) (similarly)
- ...
- A_{N-2} ≤ min(B_{N-3}, B_{N-2}) 
- A_{N-1} ≤ B_{N-2} (from constraint B_{N-2} ≥ max(A_{N-2}, A_{N-1}))

So the maximum sum is:
- A_0 = B[0]
- A_i = min(B[i-1], B[i]) for i from 1 to N-2
- A_{N-1} = B[N-2]

Therefore, sum = B[0] + sum(min(B[i-1], B[i]) for i from 1 to N-2) + B[N-2]

Let me implement this:

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

if N == 2:
    # Only two elements: A_0 and A_1
    # A_0 ≤ B[0], A_1 ≤ B[1]
    # Sum = A_0 + A_1 = B[0] + B[1]
    print(B[0] + B[1])
else:
    total_sum = B[0] + B[N-2]  # A_0 and A_{N-1}
    
    # For middle elements A_1 to A_{N-2}
    for i in range(1, N-1):
        total_sum += min(B[i-1], B[i])
    
    print(total_sum)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an integer sequence A of length N whose values are unknown.
Given is an integer sequence B of length N-1 which is known to satisfy the following:
 B_i \geq \max(A_i, A_{i+1})

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 2 \leq N \leq 100
 - 0 \leq B_i \leq 10^5

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
B_1 B_2 ... B_{N-1}

-----Output-----
Print the maximum possible sum of the elements of A.

-----Sample Input-----
3
2 5

-----Sample Output-----
9

A can be, for example, ( 2 , 1 , 5 ), ( -1 , -2 , -3 ), or ( 2 , 2 , 5 ). Among those candidates, A = ( 2 , 2 , 5 ) has the maximum possible sum.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

The constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) means that for each i from 1 to N-1:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

## Key Insight

To maximize the sum of A, we want each element A_i to be as large as possible while satisfying all constraints.

For position i:
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from constraint with B_{i-1} for i ≥ 2)
- A_i ≤ B_i (from constraint with B_i for i ≤ N-1)

Actually, let me reconsider this systematically.

For each position i (1 to N):
- A_i is constrained by B_{i-1} if i > 1 (because B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i))
- A_i is constrained by B_i if i < N (because B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

Wait, let me be more precise:
- For i from 1 to N-1: B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- This means: A_i ≤ B_i and A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

So for each A_i:
- If i = 1: A_1 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
- If i = N: A_N ≤ B_{N-1} (from B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N))
- If 1 < i < N: A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i)) AND A_i ≤ B_i (from B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

Therefore, the maximum possible value for A_i is the minimum of all B_j that constrain it:
- For A_1: only B_1 → A_1 ≤ B_1
- For A_2: B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1,A_2)) and B_2 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2,A_3)) → A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2)
- For A_3: B_2 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2,A_3)) and B_3 (from B_3 ≥ max(A_3,A_4)) → A_3 ≤ min(B_2, B_3)
- ...
- For A_i (where 1 < i < N): A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)
- For A_N: only B_{N-1} → A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

So the pattern is:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1
- A_N = B_{N-1}

Wait, is this correct? Let me verify with the example:
- N = 3, B = [2, 5]
- A_1 ≤ B_1 = 2 → A_1 ≤ 2
- A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2
- A_3 ≤ B_2 = 5 → A_3 ≤ 5

Maximum sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

This matches the expected output.

## Algorithm

1. Read N and the sequence B
2. Initialize A as an array of zeros
3. Set A[0] = B[0]
4. For i from 1 to N-2 (inclusive), set A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])
5. Set A[N-1] = B[N-2]
6. Sum all elements of A

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Initialize A array
a = [0] * n

# Set first element
a[0] = b[0]

# Set middle elements (indices 1 to n-2)
for i in range(1, n-1):
    a[i] = min(b[i-1], b[i])

# Set last element
a[n-1] = b[n-2]

# Calculate and print the sum
print(sum(a))
```

- A[1] = b[0] = 3

- This seems correct.

- Sum = 1+1+2 = 4
- Check: B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) = max(1,1) = 1 ✓
- Check: B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) = max(1,2) = 2 ✓
- The constraints are satisfied and sum is maximized.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

The constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) means that for each i from 1 to N-1:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

## Key Insight

To maximize the sum of A, we want each element A_i to be as large as possible while satisfying all constraints.

For each position i:
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from constraint with B_{i-1})
- A_i ≤ B_i (from constraint with B_i)

Wait, let me reconsider the indexing.

Actually, for each position i (1 ≤ i ≤ N-1), we have:
- B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})

This means:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

So for position i, the constraint from B_i gives us that A_i ≤ B_i.

For position i+1, the constraint from B_{i+1} gives us that A_{i+1} ≤ B_{i+1} (if i+1 ≤ N-1).

But we also have A_{i+1} ≤ B_i from the constraint with B_i.

Therefore, for each position j, the maximum value A_j can take is limited by:
- A_j ≤ B_{j-1} (from constraint with B_{j-1}, when j ≥ 2)
- A_j ≤ B_j (from constraint with B_j)

For position 1: only constraint is A_1 ≤ B_1
For position N: only constraint is A_N ≤ B_{N-1}
For position j (2 ≤ j ≤ N-1): A_j ≤ min(B_{j-1}, B_j)

Wait, that's not quite right. Let me trace through the constraints systematically:

- For position 1: A_1 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
- For position 2: A_2 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2)) and A_2 ≤ B_2 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3))
- For position 3: A_3 ≤ B_2 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3)) and A_3 ≤ B_3 (from B_3 ≥ max(A_3, A_4))

Actually, for position j (where 1 ≤ j ≤ N-1), we have A_j ≤ B_j (from B_j ≥ max(A_j, A_{j+1})).

For position j where j ≥ 2, we also have A_j ≤ B_{j-1} (from B_{j-1} ≥ max(A_{j-1}, A_j)).

So for position j:
- A_j ≤ B_j
- If j ≥ 2, then A_j ≤ B_{j-1}

Therefore, A_j ≤ min(B_j, B_{j-1}) when j ≥ 2, and A_1 ≤ B_1.

But wait, let me check this with the example:
- N = 3, B = [2, 5]
- A_1 ≤ B_1 = 2
- A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2
- A_3 ≤ B_2 = 5

So maximum sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9. This matches the expected output!

Let me verify the constraints:
- B_1 = 2 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) = max(2, 2) = 2 ✓
- B_2 = 5 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) = max(2, 5) = 5 ✓

Perfect!

## General Solution

For each position i (1 ≤ i ≤ N):
- If i = 1: A_1 ≤ B_1
- If i = N: A_N ≤ B_{N-1}
- If 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1: A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

Therefore, to maximize the sum, we set:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1
- A_N = B_{N-1}

Wait, the above is correct for positions 2 to N-1, but let me double-check:

For position 2 in our example: min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2. This works.

Actually, for position i (where i ≥ 2), we have:
- Constraint from B_{i-1}: A_i ≤ B_{i-1}
- Constraint from B_i: A_i ≤ B_i

So A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i).

For position 1: only constraint from B_1: A_1 ≤ B_1
For position N: only constraint from B_{N-1}: A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

So the maximum values are:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1  
- A_N = B_{N-1}

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate maximum possible sum
total_sum = 0

# First element A_1: constrained by B_1
total_sum += B[0]

# Middle elements A_2 to A_{N-1}: constrained by both B_{i-1} and B_i
for i in range(1, N-1):
    total_sum += min(B[i-1], B[i])

# Last element A_N: constrained by B_{N-1}
total_sum += B[N-2]

print(total_sum)
```

Wait, let me check the indexing again:

- B has indices 0 to N-2
- We want A_1, A_2, ..., A_N

For A_1: constraint from B[0] → A_1 ≤ B[0]
For A_2: constraints from B[0] and B[1] → A_2 ≤ min(B[0], B[1])
...
For A_{N-1}: constraints from B[N-2] and B[N-2] → wait no

- A_2 uses B[0] and B[1]

- ...
- A_{N-1} uses B[N-3] and B[N-2]
- A_N uses only B[N-2]

So for positions 2 to N-1 (in 1-indexing), which is indices 1 to N-2 in 0-indexing, we have:
- A_{i} for i from 2 to N-1 → index i-1 from 1 to N-2
- A_{i} ≤ min(B[i-2], B[i-1])

For position i (1-indexed), its constraints come from:
- B_{i-1} (when i ≥ 2) → B[i-2] in 0-indexing
- B_i (when i ≤ N-1) → B[i-1] in 0-indexing

So for position i (1 to N):
- A_1: ≤ B[0]
- A_i for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1: ≤ min(B[i-2], B[i-1])
- A_N: ≤ B[N-2]

The loop should be for i from 1 to N-2 (0-indexed), which corresponds to positions 2 to N-1 (1-indexed).

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

## Key Insight

For each pair (A_i, A_{i+1}), we know that B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}).

To maximize the sum of A, we want each A_i to be as large as possible while satisfying all constraints.

For position i (where 1 ≤ i ≤ N-1), we have:
- B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i)
- B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})

This means A_i ≤ B_{i-1} and A_i ≤ B_i.

So the maximum value we can assign to A_i is min(B_{i-1}, B_i).

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, for A_i, it needs to satisfy:
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from constraint with A_{i-1})
- A_i ≤ B_i (from constraint with A_{i+1})

So indeed, A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 1 to N-1.

But what about the endpoints?
- For A_1: Only constraint is A_1 ≤ B_1 (since B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
- For A_N: Only constraint is A_N ≤ B_{N-1} (since B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N))

For A_1: we have A_1 ≤ B_1, and since there's no constraint from left, A_1 can be as large as B_1.
For A_N: we have A_N ≤ B_{N-1}, so A_N can be as large as B_{N-1}.
For A_i (where 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1): we have A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i).

## Verification with sample

Sample: N=3, B=[2,5]
- A_1 ≤ B_1 = 2
- A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2) = min(2,5) = 2  
- A_3 ≤ B_2 = 5

Maximum sum = A_1 + A_2 + A_3 = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

This matches the expected output.

## General Solution

For each position i:
- If i = 1: A_1 ≤ B_1, so maximum A_1 = B_1
- If i = N: A_N ≤ B_{N-1}, so maximum A_N = B_{N-1}
- If 1 < i < N: A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i), so maximum A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

Wait, let me double-check this. For position i, we need:
- A_i ≤ max(A_{i-1}, A_i) ≤ B_{i-1} → A_i ≤ B_{i-1}
- A_i ≤ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) ≤ B_i → A_i ≤ B_i

So A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i).

Actually, for i=1 (first element):
- Only constraint is from B_1: max(A_1, A_2) ≤ B_1 → A_1 ≤ B_1
- So A_1 ≤ B_1

For i=N (last element):
- Only constraint is from B_{N-1}: max(A_{N-1}, A_N) ≤ B_{N-1} → A_N ≤ B_{N-1}
- So A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

For 1 < i < N:
- From B_{i-1}: max(A_{i-1}, A_i) ≤ B_{i-1} → A_i ≤ B_{i-1}
- From B_i: max(A_i, A_{i+1}) ≤ B_i → A_i ≤ B_i
- So A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

Therefore, the maximum sum is:
A_1 + A_2 + ... + A_N = B_1 + min(B_1, B_2) + min(B_2, B_3) + ... + min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1}) + B_{N-1}

Wait, let me write this more clearly:

Sum = A_1 + A_2 + ... + A_N
    = B_1 + min(B_1, B_2) + min(B_2, B_3) + ... + min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1}) + B_{N-1}

Actually, let me verify indices:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_2 = min(B_1, B_2) 
- A_3 = min(B_2, B_3)
- ...
- A_{N-1} = min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1})
- A_N = B_{N-1}

So the sum is: B_1 + min(B_1, B_2) + min(B_2, B_3) + ... + min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1}) + B_{N-1}

But wait, the middle terms should be min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 2 to N-1.

Actually, the pattern is:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_2 = min(B_1, B_2)
- A_3 = min(B_2, B_3)
- ...
- A_{N-1} = min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1})
- A_N = B_{N-1}

So sum = B_1 + min(B_1, B_2) + min(B_2, B_3) + ... + min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1}) + B_{N-1}

This can be written as:
sum = B_1 + B_{N-1} + Σ(min(B_{i}, B_{i+1}) for i from 1 to N-2)

Wait, let me check the indices. The middle terms are min(B_i, B_{i+1}) for i from 1 to N-2.

Actually, from above:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_2 = min(B_1, B_2)
- A_3 = min(B_2, B_3)
- ...
- A_{N-1} = min(B_{N-2}, B_{N-1})
- A_N = B_{N-1}

So the sum is: B_1 + Σ(min(B_i, B_{i+1}) for i from 1 to N-2) + B_{N-1}

For N=3: B_1 + min(B_1, B_2) + B_2 = 2 + min(2,5) + 5 = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

This matches.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n == 2:
    # Only two elements: A_1 ≤ b_0, A_2 ≤ b_0
    # So max sum = b_0 + b_0 = 2 * b_0
    print(2 * b[0])
else:
    # General case
    total = b[0]  # A_1 = b[0]
    
    # For positions 2 to N-1 (indices 1 to N-2 in b)
    for i in range(n - 2):
        total += min(b[i], b[i + 1])
    
    # For last element A_N = b[N-2]
    total += b[n - 2]
    
    print(total)
```

Wait, let me verify the indices again.

For N=3: b = [b0, b1]
- A1 = b0
- A2 = min(b0, b1)
- A3 = b1
- Sum = b0 + min(b0, b1) + b1

But in my code above, for n=3:
- total starts with b[0]
- loop for i from 0 to n-3 = 0 to 0, so i=0: add min(b[0], b[1])
- then add b[n-2] = b[1]

So total = b[0] + min(b[0], b[1]) + b[1] ✓

This is correct.

```python
n = int(input())

# For positions 2 to N-1 (which correspond to indices 1 to N-2 in the original array)
for i in range(n - 2):
    total += min(b[i], b[i + 1])

# For last element A_N = b[N-2]
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an integer sequence A of length N whose values are unknown.
Given is an integer sequence B of length N-1 which is known to satisfy the following:
 B_i \geq \max(A_i, A_{i+1}) 
Find the maximum possible sum of the elements of A.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 2 \leq N \leq 100
 - 0 \leq B_i \leq 10^5

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
B_1 B_2 ... B_{N-1}

-----Output-----
Print the maximum possible sum of the elements of A.

-----Sample Input-----
3
2 5

-----Sample Output-----
9

A can be, for example, ( 2 , 1 , 5 ), ( -1 , -2 , -3 ), or ( 2 , 2 , 5 ). Among those candidates, A = ( 2 , 2 , 5 ) has the maximum possible sum.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

## Key Insights

1. For each position i, we know that B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
2. This means both A_i ≤ B_i and A_{i+1} ≤ B_i
3. To maximize the sum of A, we want each A_i to be as large as possible while satisfying all constraints

## Strategy

For each position i in A:
- A_i must be ≤ B_{i-1} (from constraint with B_{i-1} for i > 1)
- A_i must be ≤ B_i (from constraint with B_i for i < N-1)
- Actually, looking at the constraint: B_j ≥ max(A_j, A_{j+1})

So for position i:
- If i = 1: A_1 ≤ B_1 (from constraint B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
- If i = N: A_N ≤ B_{N-1} (from constraint B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N))
- For middle positions: A_i ≤ B_{i-1} and A_i ≤ B_i

Wait, let me re-examine the constraint:
- B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})

This means:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

So each A_i can be at most B_{i-1} and B_i, but we need to find the maximum values.

Actually, for position i:
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from constraint B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i))
- A_i ≤ B_i (from constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

So A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

But wait - constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) means A_i ≤ B_i and A_{i+1} ≤ B_i.

For position 1: only constraint is A_1 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
For position N: only constraint is A_N ≤ B_{N-1} (from B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N))
For position i (1 < i < N): A_i ≤ B_{i-1} and A_i ≤ B_i, so A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

But is this the maximum? Actually, we can make A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for internal positions, and similarly for edges.

Let me verify with the example:
- N = 3, B = [2, 5]
- We need B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) → 2 ≥ max(A_1, A_2)
- We need B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) → 5 ≥ max(A_2, A_3)

To maximize sum A_1 + A_2 + A_3:
- A_1 ≤ 2 (from first constraint)
- A_2 ≤ 2 (from first constraint) and A_2 ≤ 5 (from second constraint) → A_2 ≤ 2
- A_3 ≤ 5 (from second constraint)

Maximum values: A_1 = 2, A_2 = 2, A_3 = 5
Sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

This matches the expected output.

So the algorithm:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 2 to N-1
- A_N = B_{N-1}

Wait, is A_1 = B_1 the maximum? Yes, because A_1 ≤ B_1.
Similarly, A_N ≤ B_{N-1}, so maximum is B_{N-1}.
For internal positions, A_i ≤ B_{i-1} and A_i ≤ B_i, so maximum is min(B_{i-1}, B_i).

Let me verify:
- Position 1: A_1 ≤ B_1 → max = B_1
- Position 2: A_2 ≤ B_1 and A_2 ≤ B_2 → max = min(B_1, B_2)
- Position 3: A_3 ≤ B_2 → max = B_2

In the example: A_1 = 2, A_2 = min(2,5) = 2, A_3 = 5 → sum = 9 ✓

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Initialize A array
A = [0] * N

# First element: A_1 <= B_1, so maximum is B_1
A[0] = B[0]

# Middle elements: A_i <= min(B_{i-1}, B_i)
for i in range(1, N-1):
    A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])

# Last element: A_N <= B_{N-1}, so maximum is B_{N-1}
A[N-1] = B[N-2]

# Calculate and print the sum
print(sum(A))
```

Wait, I see a potential issue. For position i in the middle, is A_i really constrained by both B_{i-1} and B_i?

Let's check the constraints:
- B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i) → A_i ≤ B_{i-1}
- B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) → A_i ≤ B_i

Yes, so A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i). So the maximum value is min(B_{i-1}, B_i).

The solution looks correct. Let me implement it:

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

# A[0] = B[0] because B[0] >= max(A[0], A[1]) so A[0] <= B[0]
# A[N-1] = B[N-2] because B[N-2] >= max(A[N-2], A[N-1]) so A[N-1] <= B[N-2]
# For i from 1 to N-2: A[i] <= min(B[i-1], B[i]) because
# B[i-1] >= max(A[i-1], A[i]) so A[i] <= B[i-1]
# and B[i] >= max(A[i], A[i+1]) so A[i] <= B[i]

A = [0] * N
A[0] = B[0]
for i in range(1, N-1):
    A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])
A[N-1] = B[N-2]

print(sum(A))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

## Key Insight

For each position i, we know that B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}). This means:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

To maximize the sum of A, we want each A_i to be as large as possible while satisfying all constraints.

## Strategy

For each position i, we have two constraints:
1. A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i))
2. A_i ≤ B_i (from B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

Actually, let me reconsider the constraints more carefully.

For position i, it appears in two B constraints:
- B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i) → A_i ≤ B_{i-1}
- B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) → A_i ≤ B_i

So A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

But we also have to consider that A_i needs to satisfy the constraints with its neighbors.

Wait, let me think differently. For each position i (from 1 to N-1), we have:
- B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})

This gives us:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

To maximize the sum, we want to set each A_i as large as possible, which means:
- A_i should be as large as possible while being ≤ B_i and also satisfying the constraint with A_{i+1}

For position 1: A_1 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
For position 2: A_2 ≤ B_1 and A_2 ≤ B_2 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) and B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3))
For position 3: A_3 ≤ B_2 and A_3 ≤ B_3 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) and B_3 ≥ max(A_3, A_4))

Actually, let me be more systematic:

For each position i (1 ≤ i ≤ N):
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} if i ≥ 2 (since B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i))
- A_i ≤ B_i if i ≤ N-1 (since B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

So for position 1: A_1 ≤ B_1
For position i (2 ≤ i ≤ N-1): A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)
For position N: A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

Wait, that's not quite right. For position N, we only have B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N), so A_N ≤ B_{N-1}.

For position 1, we have B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2), so A_1 ≤ B_1.

For position 2, we have B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) and B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3), so A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2).

- A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2  
- A_3 ≤ B_2 = 5

The constraint for A_i is actually A_i ≤ B_{i-1} when i ≥ 2, and A_i ≤ B_i when i ≤ N-1.

- If i = 1: A_1 ≤ B_1
- If 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1: A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) 
- If i = N: A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

But for i=1: we also have A_1 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
For i=2: we have A_2 ≤ B_1 (from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2)) and A_2 ≤ B_2 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3))
For i=3: we have A_3 ≤ B_2 (from B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3))

So the maximum value for A_i is:
- A_1 ≤ B_1
- A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1
- A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

Actually, for position i, it must satisfy both constraints, so A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1} if i ≥ 2, B_i if i ≤ N-1).

For i=1: only constraint from B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) → A_1 ≤ B_1
For i=N: only constraint from B_{N-1} ≥ max(A_{N-1}, A_N) → A_N ≤ B_{N-1}
For 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1: constraint from B_{i-1} and B_i → A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)

Therefore, we can set:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for 2 ≤ i ≤ N-1

A_2 = min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2

Sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9 ✓

This makes sense because:
- B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) = max(2, 2) = 2 → 2 ≥ 2 ✓
- B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) = max(2, 5) = 5 → 5 ≥ 5 ✓

## Solution

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

if N == 2:
    # Only two elements, A1 and A2
    # A1 <= B1, A2 <= B1 (from B1 >= max(A1,A2))
    # Also A2 <= B1 from B1 >= max(A1,A2)
    # So A1 can be B1, A2 can be B1
    # But wait: B1 >= max(A1, A2), so both A1 and A2 <= B1
    # To maximize sum, set A1 = B1, A2 = B1
    # Sum = 2 * B1
    print(2 * B[0])
else:
    # For general N
    # A1 = B[0]
    # For i from 1 to N-2: A_{i+1} = min(B[i], B[i+1]) 
    # A_N = B[N-2]
    
    # Actually, let's be more systematic:
    # A[0] = B[0]
    # For i from 1 to N-2: A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])
    # A[N-1] = B[N-2]
    
    A = [0] * N
    A[0] = B[0]
    for i in range(1, N-1):
        A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])
    A[N-1] = B[N-2]
    
    print(sum(A))
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me simplify:

For each position i:
- A_i can be at most min(B_{i-1}, B_i) when i >= 1 and i <= N-1
- But for the first element: A_0 can be at most B_0 (since B_0 >= max(A_0, A_1))
- For the last element: A_{N-1} can be at most B_{N-2} (since B_{N-2} >= max(A_{N-2}, A_{N-1}))

But actually, for position i (0-indexed):
- A_i <= B_{i-1} if i > 0 (from B_{i-1} >= max(A_{i-1}, A_i))
- A_i <= B_i if i < N-1 (from B_i >= max(A_i, A_{i+1}))

So A_i <= min(B_{i-1}, B_i) when 0 < i < N-1
A_0 <= B_0
A_{N-1} <= B_{N-2}

Wait, no. For i=0: A_0 <= B_0 (since B_0 >= max(A_0, A_1))
For i=1: B_0 >= max(A_0, A_1) and B_1 >= max(A_1, A_2), so A_1 <= min(B_0, B_1)
For i=2: A_2 <= min(B_1, B_2) 
...
For i=N-2: A_{N-2} <= min(B_{N-3}, B_{N-2})
For i=N-1: A_{N-1} <= B_{N-2} (since B_{N-2} >= max(A_{N-2}, A_{N-1}))

Actually, the constraint for A_i is:
- From B_{i-1} ≥ max(A_{i-1}, A_i): A_i ≤ B_{i-1} when i ≥ 1
- From B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}): A_i ≤ B_i when i ≤ N-2

So for position i (0-indexed):
- If i = 0: A_0 ≤ B_0
- If 1 ≤ i ≤ N-2: A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i)
- If i = N-1: A_{N-1} ≤ B_{N-2}

Therefore:
- A[0] = B[0]
- A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i]) for 1 ≤ i ≤ N-2
- A[N-1] = B[N-2]

```python
N = int(input())
B = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Initialize A with maximum possible values
A = [0] * N

# First element: A[0] <= B[0]
A[0] = B[0]

# Middle elements: A[i] <= min(B[i-1], B[i]) for 1 <= i <= N-2
for i in range(1, N-1):
    A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])

# Last element: A[N-1] <= B[N-2]
A[N-1] = B[N-2]

print(sum(A))
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

There is an integer sequence A of length N whose values are unknown.
Given is an integer sequence B of length N-1 which is known to satisfy the following:
 B_i \geq \max(A_i, A_{i+1}) 
Find the maximum possible sum of the elements of A.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 2 \leq N \leq 100
 - 0 \leq B_i \leq 10^5

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
B_1 B_2 ... B_{N-1}

-----Output-----
Print the maximum possible sum of the elements of A.

-----Sample Input-----
3
2 5

-----Sample Output-----
9

A can be, for example, ( 2 , 1 , 5 ), ( -1 , -2 , -3 ), or ( 2 , 2 , 5 ). Among those candidates, A = ( 2 , 2 , 5 ) has the maximum possible sum.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have:
- An unknown sequence A of length N
- A known sequence B of length N-1 where B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1})
- We need to find the maximum possible sum of elements in A

The constraint B_i ≥ max(A_i, A_{i+1}) means that for each i from 1 to N-1:
- A_i ≤ B_i
- A_{i+1} ≤ B_i

## Key Insight

To maximize the sum of A, we want each element A_i to be as large as possible, while satisfying the constraints.

For position i, we have two constraints:
- A_i ≤ B_{i-1} (from the constraint involving B_{i-1})
- A_i ≤ B_i (from the constraint involving B_i)

Therefore, A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 2 to N-1.

For the first element A_1:
- A_1 ≤ B_1 (from the constraint involving B_1)
- There's no constraint from B_0

For the last element A_N:
- A_N ≤ B_{N-1} (from the constraint involving B_{N-1})
- There's no constraint from B_{N}

So the constraints are:
- A_1 ≤ B_1
- A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 2 to N-1
- A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

Wait, let me reconsider. For A_1, the only constraint is from B_1 since B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2). So A_1 ≤ B_1.

For A_2, we have two constraints:
- From B_1: A_2 ≤ B_1 (since B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2))
- From B_2: A_2 ≤ B_2 (since B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3))

So A_2 ≤ min(B_1, B_2)

For A_3, we have:
- From B_2: A_3 ≤ B_2 (since B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3))
- From B_3: A_3 ≤ B_3 (since B_3 ≥ max(A_3, A_4))

So A_3 ≤ min(B_2, B_3)

Following this pattern:
- A_1 ≤ B_1
- A_i ≤ min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 2 to N-1
- A_N ≤ B_{N-1}

Actually, for A_N, we only have the constraint from B_{N-1}: A_N ≤ B_{N-1}.

So the maximum possible values are:
- A_1 = B_1
- A_i = min(B_{i-1}, B_i) for i from 2 to N-1
- A_N = B_{N-1}

Wait, let me verify with the sample:
- N = 3, B = [2, 5]
- A_1 = B_1 = 2
- A_2 = min(B_1, B_2) = min(2, 5) = 2
- A_3 = B_2 = 5
- Sum = 2 + 2 + 5 = 9

This matches the expected output!

Let me verify if this assignment satisfies the constraints:
- B_1 ≥ max(A_1, A_2) = max(2, 2) = 2 ✓ (2 ≥ 2)
- B_2 ≥ max(A_2, A_3) = max(2, 5) = 5 ✓ (5 ≥ 5)

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. Read N and the sequence B of length N-1
2. Initialize A as an array of length N
3. Set A[0] = B[0]
4. For i from 1 to N-2 (inclusive), set A[i] = min(B[i-1], B[i])
5. Set A[N-1] = B[N-2]
6. Return sum of A

Actually, let me simplify and implement it directly.

```python

b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Initialize A with length n

# First element A[0] can be at most B[0]

    a[i] = min(b[i-1], b[i])

a[n-1] = b[n-2]
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have a two-dimensional grid with H \times W squares. There are M targets to destroy in this grid - the position of the i-th target is \left(h_i, w_i \right).
Takahashi will choose one square in this grid, place a bomb there, and ignite it. The bomb will destroy all targets that are in the row or the column where the bomb is placed. It is possible to place the bomb at a square with a target.
Takahashi is trying to maximize the number of targets to destroy. Find the maximum number of targets that can be destroyed.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq H, W \leq 3 \times 10^5
 - 1 \leq M \leq \min\left(H\times W, 3 \times 10^5\right)
 - 1 \leq h_i \leq H
 - 1 \leq w_i \leq W
 - \left(h_i, w_i\right) \neq \left(h_j, w_j\right) \left(i \neq j\right)

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W M
h_1 w_1
\vdots
h_M w_M

-----Output-----
Print the answer.

-----Sample Input-----
2 3 3
2 2
1 1
1 3

3

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an H×W grid with M targets at specific positions
2. We place a bomb at one square (h, w)
3. The bomb destroys all targets in the same row h OR same column w
4. We want to maximize the number of targets destroyed

## Key Insight

When we place a bomb at position (r, c), it destroys:
- All targets in row r
- All targets in column c

But if there's a target at (r, c), it would be counted twice - once in row r and once in column c.

So the total targets destroyed = (targets in row r) + (targets in column c) - (1 if there's a target at (r,c) else 0)

Therefore, for each possible bomb position (r, c), we need to:
1. Count targets in row r
2. Count targets in column c  
3. Check if there's a target at (r, c)
4. Calculate: row_count[r] + col_count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c) else 0)

## Approach

1. Read the input and store target positions
2. Count targets in each row and each column
3. For each possible target position, calculate how many targets would be destroyed if we place the bomb there
4. The answer is the maximum value among all these calculations

However, we don't need to check every possible position in the grid (which could be up to 3×10^5 × 3×10^5). We only need to check positions where there are targets, because:
- If we place the bomb at a position with no target, it will destroy exactly row_count[r] + col_count[c] targets
- If we place it at a position with a target, it will destroy row_count[r] + col_count[c] - 1 targets
- The maximum will likely occur at positions with targets, or at target positions that maximize the sum

Actually, we need to be more careful. The optimal position might be at a target position or at a blank position.

But since we want to maximize row_count[r] + col_count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c)), and row_count and col_count are fixed, we should consider:
- All positions (r, c) where there's a target
- All positions (r, c) that could potentially maximize the sum

However, since row_count[r] and col_count[c] are fixed for each row and column, the maximum value of row_count[r] + col_count[c] will occur at positions where both row_count[r] and col_count[c] are large.

The key insight is that we only need to check all target positions, because:
- For any position without a target, the destruction count is row_count[r] + col_count[c]
- For any position with a target, the destruction count is row_count[r] + col_count[c] - 1
- If we find a candidate at a target position, it's equivalent to having row_count[r] + col_count[c] - 1, which is less than row_count[r] + col_count[c]
- So the maximum must occur at a position where row_count[r] + col_count[c] is maximized

But we need to check if a position without a target can give us a larger sum than any target position.

Actually, we can optimize: we just need to find the maximum value of row_count[r] + col_count[c] over all possible r and c, and then subtract 1 if there's a target at that position.

Wait, that's not quite right. We don't know where the maximum row_count[r] + col_count[c] occurs.

Simple approach:
1. Count targets per row and per column
2. For each target position (h_i, w_i), calculate: row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] - 1
3. Also, we should check if there's a position (r, c) that maximizes row_count[r] + col_count[c], and if that position has no target, then we can get row_count[r] + col_count[c]
4. But actually, since we don't know which position has the maximum sum, we can just find the maximum possible row_count[r] + col_count[c], and then check if there's any target at that position.

Actually, the simplest approach is to:
- Count row counts and column counts
- For each target position, calculate how many targets it would destroy
- The answer is the maximum of these values

But is this sufficient? Consider: what if placing the bomb at a position with no target gives more destruction than any target position?

For example, if we have a row with 5 targets and a column with 5 targets, and there's no target at their intersection, then placing the bomb there gives 10 targets destroyed. But if there is a target at their intersection, and the row has 6 targets and column has 6 targets, then placing the bomb at the intersection gives 11 targets destroyed (6+6-1).

The key is that the total destruction at (r,c) is row_count[r] + col_count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c) else 0).

So we need to consider all possible (r,c) positions, but since H and W can be up to 3×10^5, we can't iterate over all positions.

However, the maximum of row_count[r] + col_count[c] will occur at some specific (r,c). We can find this maximum by considering the maximum row_count and maximum col_count.

Let max_row = maximum of row_count[r] for all r
Let max_col = maximum of col_count[c] for all c

Then the maximum possible row_count[r] + col_count[c] is max_row + max_col.

Now, if there exists a position (r,c) where row_count[r] = max_row, col_count[c] = max_col, and there is NO target at (r,c), then we can get max_row + max_col targets destroyed.

Otherwise, if every position where row_count[r] = max_row and col_count[c] = max_col has a target, then we get max_row + max_col - 1.

But actually, we might have multiple positions with row_count[r] = max_row and col_count[c] = max_col. We need to check if there's any such position without a target.

Since we only have M targets, and M ≤ 3×10^5, we can check this.

Actually, even simpler: we don't need to go through all positions. We can:

1. Count row_count and col_count
2. Find all positions (r,c) where row_count[r] = max_row and col_count[c] = max_col
3. Check if any of these positions has no target
4. If yes, answer is max_row + max_col
5. If no, answer is max_row + max_col - 1

But we also need to consider that maybe a target position gives a better result than the maximum possible row_count[r] + col_count[c] - 1.

For a target position (h_i, w_i), the destruction count is row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] - 1.

So we need to compare:
- max_row + max_col (if achievable at a position without target)
- max over all target positions of (row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] - 1)

Actually, max_row + max_col could be larger than any row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i], so we need to consider both.

Therefore, the answer is:
max( max_row + max_col if there exists (r,c) with row_count[r]=max_row, col_count[c]=max_col and no target at (r,c), 
      max over all target positions of (row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] - 1) )

Wait, this is getting complex. Let me reconsider.

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating. Since we want to maximize row_count[r] + col_count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c)), and we can place the bomb anywhere, the optimal strategy is to place it at a position (r,c) that maximizes row_count[r] + col_count[c], and if there's a target at that position, we subtract 1.

The maximum of row_count[r] + col_count[c] is max_row + max_col.

Now, if there exists any position (r,c) where row_count[r] = max_row, col_count[c] = max_col, and there is NO target at (r,c), then placing the bomb there gives max_row + max_col targets.

Otherwise, if every position with row_count[r] = max_row and col_count[c] = max_col has a target, then we can't get max_row + max_col, the best we can get is max_row + max_col - 1 (by placing at one of those positions).

But we also need to check if any target position gives a value greater than max_row + max_col - 1.

For a target position (h_i, w_i), we get row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] - 1.

This could potentially be greater than max_row + max_col - 1 only if row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] > max_row + max_col, which is impossible since row_count[h_i] ≤ max_row and col_count[w_i] ≤ max_col.

So row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] ≤ max_row + max_col, so row_count[h_i] + col_count[w_i] - 1 ≤ max_row + max_col - 1.

Therefore, the maximum is:
- max_row + max_col, if there exists a position (r,c) with row_count[r] = max_row, col_count[c] = max_col, and no target at (r,c)
- max_row + max_col - 1, otherwise

Therefore, the algorithm:

1. Read input
2. Count targets per row and per column
3. Find max_row = max(row_count[i] for i in 1..H)
4. Find max_col = max(col_count[j] for j in 1..W)
5. For each row r where row_count[r] = max_row and each column c where col_count[c] = max_col, check if there's no target at (r,c)
6. If any such position has no target, answer = max_row + max_col
7. Otherwise, answer = max_row + max_col - 1

But wait - is that correct? Consider if there's a position with row_count[r] = max_row and col_count[c] = max_col, and no target, then placing the bomb there gives max_row + max_col targets. 

Yes.

But what if there's a target position that gives more than max_row + max_col - 1? As argued above, no, because row_count[r] + col_count[c] ≤ max_row + max_col for any (r,c).

Therefore, this should work.

Let me verify with the sample:
H=2, W=3, M=3
Targets: (2,2), (1,1), (1,3)

Row counts: row1: 2, row2: 1 → max_row = 2
Column counts: col1: 1, col2: 1, col3: 1 → max_col = 1

So max_row + max_col = 3

Now, positions where row_count[r]=2 (only row1) and col_count[c]=1 (all columns): (1,1), (1,2), (1,3)

Do any of these have no target? 
- (1,1) has target → yes
- (1,2) has no target → yes
- (1,3) has target → yes

Since (1,2) has no target, we can achieve max_row + max_col = 3.

Answer is 3. ✓

Another example: if all intersections of max row and max col have targets, then answer = max_row + max_col - 1.

This seems correct.

```python
H, W, M = map(int, input().split())
targets = []
row_count = [0] * (H + 1)
col_count = [0] * (W + 1)
target_set = set()

for _ in range(M):
    h, w = map(int, input().split())
    targets.append((h, w))
    row_count[h] += 1
    col_count[w] += 1
    target_set.add((h, w))

# Find max row count and max col count
max_row = max(row_count[1:])
max_col = max(col_count[1:])

# Check if there exists a position (r,c) where row_count[r] == max_row, col_count[c] == max_col, and (r,c) not in target_set
found_no_target = False
for r in range(1, H + 1):
    if row_count[r] == max_row:
        for c in range(1, W + 1):
            if col_count[c] == max_col:
                if (r, c) not in target_set:
                    found_no_target = True
                    break
        if found_no_target:
            break

if found_no_target:
    print(max_row + max_col)
else:
    print(max_row + max_col - 1)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an H×W grid with M targets at specific positions
2. We place a bomb at one square (h, w)
3. The bomb destroys all targets in the same row h OR same column w
4. We want to maximize the number of targets destroyed

## Key Insight

When we place a bomb at position (h, w), it destroys:
- All targets in row h
- All targets in column w

However, if there's a target at position (h, w) itself, it would be counted twice (in both row and column), so we need to be careful.

The total targets destroyed = (targets in row h) + (targets in column w) - (1 if there's a target at (h,w) else 0)

## Strategy

For each possible bomb position (h, w), we need to:
1. Count targets in row h

Actually, we can:
- Let A = set of rows with row_count[h] = max_row_count
- Let B = set of columns with col_count[w] = max_col_count
- The positions we care about are A × B
- We need to know if there exists (h,w) in A×B such that (h,w) is not in target_set

This is equivalent to: (A×B) - target_set is not empty.

So if |A×B| > |A×B ∩ target_set|, then answer is max_row_count + max_col_count
Otherwise, answer is max_row_count + max_col_count - 1

|A×B| = |A| × |B|
|A×B ∩ target_set| is the number of targets that lie in rows with max_row_count and columns with max_col_count.

We can compute this by:
- For each target (h,w), if row_count[h] == max_row_count and col_count[w] == max_col_count, then it contributes to the intersection.

So we can iterate through all targets and count how many have both properties.

Then:
- If (|A| × |B|) > (count of targets with both properties), then answer = max_row_count + max_col_count
- Else, answer = max_row_count + max_col_count - 1

Let me verify with the sample:

Row counts: row1=2, row2=1 → max_row_count=2
Col counts: col1=1, col2=1, col3=1 → max_col_count=1

A = {1} (only row 1 has count 2)
B = {1,2,3} (all columns have count 1)

A×B has 3 positions: (1,1), (1,2), (1,3)
Targets at these positions: (1,1), (1,3) → so 2 targets in A×B
Count of targets with both properties = 2

|A|×|B| = 1×3 = 3
3 > 2, so answer = 2 + 1 = 3 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

So algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Count targets by row and column
3. Find max_row_count and max_col_count
4. Find all rows with row_count == max_row_count and all columns with col_count == max_col_count
5. Count how many targets have both: row_count == max_row_count and col_count == max_col_count
6. If (|rows_with_max_row| × |cols_with_max_col|) > (count_above), then answer = max_row_count + max_col_count
7. Otherwise, answer = max_row_count + max_col_count - 1

However, what about the case where max_row_count and max_col_count are achieved by multiple rows/columns? Our reasoning still holds.

Edge case: what if no row has max_count? That can't happen since we have at least one target.

What if a row has many targets but no column has high count? Same logic.

Actually, we need to ensure we're finding the maximum values correctly.

Let me implement this.

```python
H, W, M = map(int, input().split())
targets = []
for _ in range(M):
    h, w = map(int, input().split())
    targets.append((h, w))

# Count targets by row and column
row_count = [0] * (H + 1)
col_count = [0] * (W + 1)

for h, w in targets:
    row_count[h] += 1
    col_count[w] += 1

# Find max row count and max col count
max_row_count = max(row_count[1:])
max_col_count = max(col_count[1:])

# Find all rows with max row count and all columns with max col count
rows_with_max_row = []
cols_with_max_col = []

for h in range(1, H + 1):
    if row_count[h] == max_row_count:
        rows_with_max_row.append(h)

for w in range(1, W + 1):
    if col_count[w] == max_col_count:
        cols_with_max_col.append(w)

# Count targets that are in both max row and max column
count_in_both = 0
for h, w in targets:
    if row_count[h] == max_row_count and col_count[w] == max_col_count:
        count_in_both += 1

# Total positions in the intersection of max row and max col sets
total_positions = len(rows_with_max_row) * len(cols_with_max_col)

if total_positions > count_in_both:
    print(max_row_count + max_col_count)
else:
    print(max_row_count + max_col_count - 1)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have a two-dimensional grid with H \times W squares. There are M targets to destroy in this grid - the position of the i-th target is \left(h_i, w_i \right).
Takahashi will choose one square in this grid, place a bomb there, and ignite it. The bomb will destroy all targets that are in the row or the column where the bomb is placed. It is possible to place the bomb at a square with a target.
Takahashi is trying to maximize the number of targets to destroy. Find the maximum number of targets that can be destroyed.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq H, W \leq 3 \times 10^5
 - 1 \leq M \leq \min\left(H\times W, 3 \times 10^5\right)
 - 1 \leq h_i \leq H
 - 1 \leq w_i \leq W
 - \left(h_i, w_i\right) \neq \left(h_j, w_j\right) \left(i \neq j\right)

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W M
h_1 w_1
\vdots
h_M w_M

-----Output-----
Print the answer.

-----Sample Input-----
2 3 3
2 2
1 1
1 3

-----Sample Output-----
3

We can destroy all the targets by placing the bomb at \left(1, 2\right).### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a H×W grid with M targets at specific positions (h_i, w_i)
2. When we place a bomb at position (r, c), it destroys all targets in row r OR column c
3. We want to maximize the number of targets destroyed
4. We need to find the optimal bomb position

## Key Insights

When we place a bomb at (r, c), it destroys:
- All targets in row r
- All targets in column c

But if there's a target at (r, c) itself, it gets counted only once (since it's in both row r and column c).

So the total targets destroyed = (targets in row r) + (targets in column c) - (1 if there's a target at (r,c))

So we can compute:

But we still need to know if max_sum is attainable at a non-target position.

Actually, we can do this:
- Compute S = max(row_counts[r] + col_counts[c])
- Find all pairs (r,c) where row_counts[r] + col_counts[c] = S
- If any such (r,c) has no target at (r,c), then answer is S
- Otherwise, answer is S-1

But this could be O(H×W) in worst case.

However, note that S = row_counts[r] + col_counts[c], and for this sum to be S, we need row_counts[r] to be the maximum among row_counts, and col_counts[c] to be the maximum among col_counts? Not necessarily.

Actually, no. For example, row_counts[0] = 10, row_counts[1] = 6, col_counts[0] = 9, col_counts[1] = 7. Then max sum is 10+9=19.

But if there's a row with 10 and a column with 9, that gives 19.

Actually, the maximum sum is achieved when we take the row with maximum row_count and column with maximum col_count? Not necessarily.

For example: row_counts = [10, 8], col_counts = [10, 8], max sum = 18.
But if row_counts = [10, 8], col_counts = [9, 9], max sum = 19 (from row 0, col 0 or col 1).

The maximum sum is max_r(row_counts[r]) + max_c(col_counts[c]).

Yes! Because if we take the row with the maximum row_count, say R_max, and the column with the maximum col_count, say C_max, then R_max + C_max is the maximum possible sum of row_counts[r] + col_counts[c].

Any other combination would be ≤ R_max + C_max.

So max_sum = max_row_count + max_col_count.

So the answer is:
- If there exists a position (r,c) such that row_counts[r] = max_row_count, col_counts[c] = max_col_count, and (r,c) is not a target position, then answer is max_row_count + max_col_count
- Otherwise, answer is max_row_count + max_col_count - 1

But wait - what about other combinations? Could there be a sum equal to max_row_count + max_col_count at a different position?

No, because any position (r,c) has row_counts[r] ≤ max_row_count and col_counts[c] ≤ max_col_count, so row_counts[r] + col_counts[c] ≤ max_row_count + max_col_count, and equality holds only when row_counts[r] = max_row_count and col_counts[c] = max_col_count.

So only positions where row_counts[r] = max_row_count and col_counts[c] = max_col_count can achieve the maximum sum.

Therefore, the maximum value of row_counts[r] + col_counts[c] is max_row_count + max_col_count, achieved at any position (r,c) where row_counts[r] = max_row_count and col_counts[c] = max_col_count.

Now, among these positions, if any of them does NOT have a target, then we can achieve max_row_count + max_col_count by placing the bomb there.

If ALL such positions have targets, then we get max_row_count + max_col_count - 1.

But we don't need to check all such positions - we just need to know if there exists at least one position (r,c) with row_counts[r] = max_row_count, col_counts[c] = max_col_count, and (r,c) not in the target set.

So algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Count targets per row and per column
3. Find max_row_count = max(row_counts[0..H-1]), max_col_count = max(col_counts[0..W-1])
4. max_sum = max_row_count + max_col_count
5. Check if there exists a row r with row_counts[r] == max_row_count and a column c with col_counts[c] == max_col_count such that (r,c) is not a target position
6. If such position exists, answer = max_sum
7. Otherwise, answer = max_sum - 1

But wait - is it possible that even if all positions with max row and max col have targets, we can still get a high value from a different combination?

No, because no other combination can reach max_sum.

Actually, we must consider that we might get max_sum - 1 at a position with a target, but the maximum possible value is max_sum, which can be achieved only at max row and max col positions.

So yes, the answer is either max_sum or max_sum-1.

Now, to check if there exists a position with row_counts[r] = max_row_count, col_counts[c] = max_col_count, and (r,c) not in the target set.

We can do this by:
- For all target positions, we know which rows and columns they occupy
- We need to check if there's any (r,c) with r having row_counts[r] == max_row_count, c having col_counts[c] == max_col_count, and (r,c) not in the target list.

But note: a target position is given by (h_i, w_i).

So we can:
- Find all rows with row_counts[r] == max_row_count
- Find all columns with col_counts[c] == max_col_count
- Check if there exists any (r,c) in (max_row_rows × max_col_cols) such that (r,c) is not a target

But we don't have all target positions in a set we can query easily.

We can create a set of target positions.

Let me implement:

```python
H, W, M = map(int, input().split())
targets = []
row_count = [0] * (H + 1)
col_count = [0] * (W + 1)

for _ in range(M):
    h, w = map(int, input().split())
    targets.append((h, w))
    row_count[h] += 1
    col_count[w] += 1

# Find max row count and max col count
max_row_count = max(row_count[1:H+1])
max_col_count = max(col_count[1:W+1])

max_sum = max_row_count + max_col_count

# Build set of target positions for O(1) lookup
target_set = set(targets)

# Check if there exists any (r,c) with row_count[r] == max_row_count and col_count[c] == max_col_count and (r,c) not in target_set
found_non_target = False
for r in range(1, H + 1):
    if row_count[r] == max_row_count:
        for c in range(1, W + 1):
            if col_count[c] == max_col_count and (r, c) not in target_set:
                found_non_target = True
                break
        if found_non_target:
            break

if found_non_target:
    print(max_sum)
else:
    print(max_sum - 1)
```

Wait, this is O(H×W) in worst case, which could be up to 9×10^10, too slow.

We need to optimize.

We only need to check if there exists at least one position (r,c) with row_count[r] = max_row_count, col_count[c] = max_col_count, and (r,c) not in target_set.

Instead, we can:
- Find the set of rows with max row_count
- Find the set of columns with max col_count
- Check if there's any (r,c) in the Cartesian product that is not in target_set

But the total number of rows with max_row_count might be large, and same for columns.

But note: M ≤ 3×10^5, so the number of target positions is limited.

We can do: for each row with max_row_count and each col with max_col_count, check if (r,c) is in target_set.

But if there are many rows with max_row_count and many columns with max_col_count, this could be O(M^2).

Actually, worst case: if all targets are in a small area, but we have max_row_count and max_col_count.

Actually, the worst-case number of rows with max_row_count and columns with max_col_count could be large.

But note: the sum of row_counts is M, so if many rows have the same max count, it might be large.

For example, if M is large and distributed evenly.

But M ≤ 3×10^5, so even if many rows have max_row_count, the total number is bounded.

For instance, if max_row_count = k, and there are R rows with count k, then R * k ≤ M, so R ≤ M/k.

Similarly, the total number of such positions is at most (number of max_row_rows) * (number of max_col_cols).

And since number of max_row_rows * max_row_count ≤ M, and number of max_col_cols * max_col_count ≤ M.

But max_row_count ≥ floor(M/H), and max_col_count ≥ floor(M/W).

So the product R * C ≤ (M / max_row_count) * (M / max_col_count) ≤ (M^2) / (max_row_count * max_col_count)

In worst case, if max_row_count and max_col_count are small, this could be large.

For example, if max_row_count = 1, max_col_count = 1, then R = M (since each row has 1), C = M (each column has 1), so R*C = M^2 = 9×10^10, too slow.

That's too slow.

We need a better way.

Alternative idea: the only way to have a position (r,c) with row_counts[r] = max_row_count, col_counts[c] = max_col_count and not in target_set is if there's an intersection that is not occupied.

But if every row with max_row_count intersects every column with max_col_count in a target, then all these intersections are occupied.

We can check this by seeing if the set of positions (r,c) with row_counts[r] = max_row_count and col_counts[c] = max_col_count is a subset of target_set.

But we don't want to iterate over all such positions.

Since the total number of such positions is R * C, and R and C might be large, but notice that for each row r with max_row_count, the number of columns c with max_col_count and such that (r,c) is in target_set is at most the number of targets in row r.

And similarly for columns.

But we can do: for each row r with row_count[r] = max_row_count, we want to know if there exists a column c with col_count[c] = max_col_count such that (r,c) is NOT in target_set.

We can check for each such row r: are there any columns c with col_count[c] = max_col_count such that (r,c) not in target_set?

But we can precompute for each row r, the set of columns where (r,c) has a target.

But that might be large.

Actually, we can do: for each row r with row_count[r] = max_row_count, we look at the target positions in that row.

For column c with col_count[c] = max_col_count, if there is no target at (r,c), then we found a non-target position.

So for each such row r, we can check if there exists a column c in the max_col_cols such that (r,c) not in target_set.

We can do this by:
- For each row r with row_count[r] = max_row_count, find the columns where there are targets in that row
- For each such column c in that row, if c is not in max_col_cols, skip
- Otherwise, if we find any column c in max_col_cols that is not in the target set for row r, then we have a non-target position.

But we can do: for a given row r, let target_cols_in_row_r = set of w such that (r,w) is a target.

Then, if there exists a column c in max_col_cols such that c not in target_cols_in_row_r, then (r,c) is a non-target position.

So algorithm:
- Let max_row_rows = set of rows r with row_count[r] == max_row_count
- Let max_col_cols = set of columns c with col_count[c] == max_col_count
- Let target_positions = set of (h,w)
- For each row r in max_row_rows:
    - Let cols_with_target_in_r = set of w such that (r,w) is in target_positions
    - If there exists a column c in max_col_cols such that c not in cols_with_target_in_r:
        - found_non_target = True
        - break

This is O(R * (number of max_col_cols)) in worst case, which could be large.

But note: the total number of target positions is M ≤ 3×10^5.

For each row r, the number of targets in it is at most the size of the row, but we are storing only the columns that have targets.

The total work across all rows in max_row_rows is O(M), because each target position will be processed once (when we process its row).

More precisely: for each target in row r, we add w to cols_with_target_in_row_r.

And we process each row r only if it has max_row_count.

The total number of such "target" entries across all rows with max_row_count is at most M.

But we also have to iterate over max_col_cols for each row.

If there are many max_col_cols, it could be O(M * C) where C is the number of max_col_cols.

But worst case C could be up to W, and if max_col_count is small, C could be large.

However, notice that the sum of row_counts is M, so if many rows have high counts, it's fine.

But worst case: max_row_count = 1, so each row has exactly 1 target.

Then R = M (if all rows have count 1), and C = M (if all columns have count 1).

Then for each row r, we have one target, so we need to check if there's a column c in max_col_cols that is not the one with the target.

If for row r, target is at (r, w_r), and if there exists c in max_col_cols with c ≠ w_r, then we found it.

But if all max_col_cols are {w_r} for that row, then maybe not.

In worst case, if we have to check for each of M rows, and for each of M columns, that might be O(M^2).

For M=3×10^5, M^2 = 9×10^10, too slow.

We need a more efficient way.

Alternative idea: if there exists any position (r,c) such that row_count[r] = max_row_count, col_count[c] = max_col_count, and (r,c) not in target_set, then answer = max_sum.

Otherwise, answer = max_sum - 1.

We can check this without iterating over all positions.

Note: the only positions that could have row_count[r] = max_row_count and col_count[c] = max_col_count are those that are in the intersection of the max-row set and max-col set.

The total number of such positions is |max_row_rows| * |max_col_cols|.

But if this product is large, we can't check all.

However, notice that if the intersection of the max-row set and max-col set has no targets, then we found a non-target.

But how to check if there's a non-target in the intersection without checking all?

We can do: the intersection has no targets if and only if every position (r,c) in the intersection is in target_set.

So we need to know if the set of target positions intersects the Cartesian product of max_row_rows and max_col_cols.

In other words, is there any (r,c) in (max_row_rows × max_col_cols) such that (r,c) in target_set?

If not, then we can achieve max_sum.

If yes, then we can only achieve max_sum-1.

So we need to know if the Cartesian product contains any target.

But again, the size of the Cartesian product might be large.

However, we can compute this by checking only the targets in the rows of max_row_rows.

Specifically: for each target (h,w), if h is in max_row_rows and w is in max_col_cols, then this target is in the intersection.

If there is at least one such target, then all positions in the intersection have targets (since every target in intersection is one of these)? No - there might be other positions in intersection that are not targets.

Wait - no. If a position (r,c) is in the intersection (max_row_rows × max_col_cols) and is not a target, then we have a non-target.

So the condition is: if there is ANY position in (max_row_rows × max_col_cols) that is not a target, then we have a non-target.

This is equivalent to: if the intersection of (max_row_rows × max_col_cols) and target_set is a proper subset of (max_row_rows × max_col_cols), then we have a non-target.

But we can check this by: is (max_row_rows × max_col_cols) completely covered by target_set?

That is, is every (r,c) in the product a target?

If not, then we have a non-target.

To check if every (r,c) in the product is a target, we can iterate over all targets and see if they cover the entire product.

But that would be O(M * |max_row_rows| * |max_col_cols|), which is too expensive.

We can do: for each row r in max_row_rows, and each column c in max_col_cols, if (r,c) is in target_set, then mark it.

But again, worst case O(R*C).

But note that R and C are the number of rows/cols with max count, and R*C might be large.

However, there's a key insight: the total number of target positions M is at most 3×10^5.

So if R*C > M, then the Cartesian product has more positions than targets, so there must be at least one position in the product that is not a target.

Therefore, if R*C > M, then we can achieve max_sum.

Otherwise, if R*C ≤ M, then we can check all positions in the product to see if they are all covered by targets.

So algorithm:
1. Compute row_count, col_count, target_set
2. Find max_row_count, max_col_count
3. max_sum = max_row_count + max_col_count
4. max_row_rows = [r for r in range(1, H+1) if row_count[r] == max_row_count]
5. max_col_cols = [c for c in range(1, W+1) if col_count[c] == max_col_count]
6. R = len(max_row_rows), C = len(max_col_cols)
7. If R * C > M:
8.     print(max_sum)
9. Else:
10.     found_target_in_product = False
11.     for r in max_row_rows:
12.         for c in max_col_cols:
13.             if (r, c) in target_set:
14.                 found_target_in_product = True
15.                 break
16.         if found_target_in_product:
17.             break
18.     if found_target_in_product:
19.         print(max_sum - 1)
20.     else:
21.         print(max_sum)

This works because:
- If R*C > M, then there are more positions in the intersection than targets, so at least one position in the intersection is not a target, so we can achieve max_sum.
- If R*C ≤ M, then it's possible that all positions in the intersection are targets, so we need to check.

And since R*C ≤ M, and M ≤ 3×10^5, the nested loops will be at most 3×10^5 iterations.

This is efficient.

Let me verify with the sample:
H=2, W=3, M=3
Targets: (2,2), (1,1), (1,3)

row_count: row1: 2, row2: 1 → max_row_count = 2
col_count: col1: 1, col2: 1, col3: 1 → max_col_count = 1

max_sum = 2+1 = 3

max_row_rows = [1] (since row1 has count 2)
max_col_cols = [1,2,3] (each has count 1)

R=1, C=3, R*C=3 ≤ M=3, so we need to check.

Check positions in row1 × col_cols: (1,1), (1,2), (1,3)

- (1,1): in target_set? yes
- (1,2): in target_set? no → we found a non-target

So answer = 3.

This matches expected output.

Another test: if all positions in max_row_rows × max_col_cols are targets, then we get max_sum-1.

For example, if we have targets at (1,1), (1,2), (2,1), (2,2) and H=2,W=2.

Then row_count: row1=2, row2=2 → max_row_count=2
col_count: col1=2, col2=2 → max_col_count=2
max_sum = 2+2 = 4
max_row_rows = [1,2], max_col_cols = [1,2]
R*C=4, M=4, so we need to check all 4: all are in target_set, so answer = 4-1 = 3.

Is that correct? 
If we place bomb at (1,1): destroys row1 (2 targets) and col1 (2 targets), but (1,1) counted once, so total = 2+2-1 = 3.
Similarly for any position. So yes, maximum is 3.

Perfect.

So final code:

```python
H, W, M = map(int, input().split())
target_set = set()
row_count = [0] * (H + 1)
col_count = [0] * (W + 1)

for _ in range(M):
    h, w = map(int, input().split())
    target_set.add((h, w))
    row_count[h] += 1
    col_count[w] += 1

max_row_count = max(row_count[1:H+1]) if H > 0 else 0
max_col_count = max(col_count[1:W+1]) if W > 0 else 0

max_sum = max_row_count + max_col_count

# Get rows and columns with max counts
max_row_rows = []
max_col_cols = []
for r in range(1, H + 1):
    if row_count[r] == max_row_count:
        max_row_rows.append(r)
for c in range(1, W + 1):
    if col_count[c] == max_col_count:
        max_col_cols.append(c)

R = len(max_row_rows)
C = len(max_col_cols)

if R * C > M:
    print(max_sum)
else:
    # Check if all positions in the product are targets
    all_targets_covered = True
    for r in max_row_rows:
        for c in max_col_cols:
            if (r, c) not in target_set:
                all_targets_covered = False
                break
        if not all_targets_covered:
            break
    
    if all_targets_covered:
        print(max_sum - 1)
    else:
        print(max_sum)
```

This should work efficiently because R*C ≤ M, so the nested loop runs at most M times.

Let me test with edge cases.

Edge case: no targets? But M≥1.

Edge case: single target.

H=2, W=2, M=1, target at (1,1)
row_count: [0,1,0], col_count: [0,1,0]
max_row_count = 1, max_col_count = 1
max_sum = 2
max_row_rows = [1], max_col_cols = [1]
R*C = 1 ≤ 1, so check: (1,1) in target_set? yes → all targets covered → answer = 2-1 = 1.

Is that correct? Bomb at (1,1): destroys row1 (1 target) and col1 (1 target), but (1,1) counted once, so total = 1+1-1 = 1. Yes.

Bomb at (1,2): destroys row1 (1 target) and col2 (0 targets) → total = 1. Same.

So maximum is 1, correct.

Another: bomb only in row with max count and col with max count, and (r,c) has no target.

H=2,W=2,M=1 at (1,2)
row_count: [0,1,0], col_count: [0,1,1] → max_row_count=1, max_col_count=1
max_row_rows=[1], max_col_cols=[2] (since col2 has count 1)
max_sum=2
R*C=1, check (1,2): in target_set? yes → all targets covered → answer = 1?

But if we place bomb at (1,1): destroys row1 (1 target) and col1 (0 targets) → total = 1.
Place at (1,2): destroys row1 (1 target) and col2 (1 target), but (1,2) counted once → total = 1+1-1 = 1.
Place at (2,1): destroys row2 (0) col1 (0) → 0.
Place at (2,2): destroys row2 (0) col2 (1) → 1.
So maximum is 1, correct.

But I want a case where max_sum is achievable.

Example: targets at (1,1), (1,2)
H=2,W=2,M=2
row_count: [0,2,0], col_count: [1,1,0]
max_row_count=2, max_col_count=1
max_sum=3
max_row_rows=[1], max_col_cols=[1,2] (both columns have count 1)
R*C=2 ≤ 2, check positions: (1,1) and (1,2)
(1,1): in set? yes
(1,2): in set? yes → all covered → answer = 3-1 = 2

Place bomb at (1,1): destroys row1 (2 targets) and col1 (1 target), but (1,1) counted once → total = 2+1-1 = 2
Place at (1,2): destroys row1 (2) and col2 (1), (1,2) counted once → total = 2+1-1 = 2
Place at (2,1): destroys row2 (0) col1 (1) → 1
So max is 2, correct.

Now can we achieve 3? Only if we have a position with row1 and col1 or col2 with no target, but both have targets in row1, so no.

So yes.

Therefore, the code is correct.

```python

        for c in max_col_cols:
            if (r, c) not in target_set:
                all_targets_covered = False
                break
        if not all_targets_covered:
            break
    
    if all_targets_covered:
        print(max_sum - 1)
    else:
        print(max_sum)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have a two-dimensional grid with H \times W squares. There are M targets to destroy in this grid - the position of the i-th target is \left(h_i, w_i \right).
Takahashi will choose one square in this grid, place a bomb there, and ignite it. The bomb will destroy all targets that are in the row or the column where the bomb is placed. It is possible to place the bomb at a square with a target.
Takahashi is trying to maximize the number of targets to destroy. Find the maximum number of targets that can be destroyed.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq H, W \leq 3 \times 10^5
 - 1 \leq M \leq \min\left(H\times W, 3 \times 10^5\right)
 - 1 \leq h_i \leq H
 - 1 \leq w_i \leq W
 - \left(h_i, w_i\right) \neq \left(h_j, w_j\right) \left(i \neq j\right)

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W M
h_1 w_1
\vdots
h_M w_M

-----Output-----
Print the answer.

-----Sample Input-----
2 3 3
2 2
1 1
1 3

-----Sample Output-----
3

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an H × W grid with M targets at specific positions
2. We place a bomb at one square (h, w)
3. The bomb destroys all targets that are in the same row h OR same column w
4. We want to maximize the number of targets destroyed

## Key Insight

When we place a bomb at position (h, w), it destroys:
- All targets in row h
- All targets in column w

However, if there's a target at position (h, w), it would be counted twice (once in row h, once in column w), so we need to avoid double counting.

The total targets destroyed = (targets in row h) + (targets in column w) - (1 if there's a target at (h, w) else 0)

## Strategy

1. Group targets by their row and column
2. Count how many targets are in each row and each column
3. For each possible bomb position (h, w), calculate how many targets would be destroyed
4. Find the maximum among all possibilities

However, we don't need to try all H×W positions. We only need to consider positions that have targets, or positions that might maximize destruction.

Actually, the optimal bomb placement will likely be at a position where either:
- There's a target in that row, or 
- There's a target in that column

But let's think more systematically:

The maximum destruction occurs when we choose a row r and column c that together cover the most targets. The formula is:
targets_in_row_r + targets_in_column_c - (1 if there's a target at (r,c) else 0)

We can try:
1. For each row, calculate the sum of targets in that row + targets in each column
2. For each column, calculate the sum of targets in that column + targets in each row

But this would be very expensive. Instead, we can be smarter.

Actually, we can observe that the optimal solution will either:
- Be at a position where a target exists, or
- Be at the intersection of a row and column that maximizes the sum

But since we only have M ≤ 3×10⁵ targets, we can try all rows and columns that contain targets.

## Algorithm

1. Read the targets and group them by row and column
2. For each row that has targets, and for each column that has targets, calculate the total destruction
3. Also consider placing the bomb at a position where there's no target (though this might not be optimal)
4. Return the maximum

Actually, we don't need to try every combination. We can:
- For each row i, the destruction if we place bomb at any position in row i would be: (count_of_targets_in_row_i) + (max_count_in_any_column)
- For each column j, the destruction if we place bomb at any position in column j would be: (count_of_targets_in_column_j) + (max_count_in_any_row)

Wait, that's not quite right either.

Let me reconsider. When we place a bomb at (r,c):
- It destroys all targets in row r (count[r])
- It destroys all targets in column c (count[c])
- But if there's a target at (r,c), it's counted twice, so we subtract 1

So total = count[r] + count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c) else 0)

We need to maximize this over all possible (r,c).

Since H and W can be up to 3×10⁵, we can't iterate over all positions. But we only need to consider positions (r,c) where either r has targets or c has targets, because if both r and c have no targets, then count[r] = 0 and count[c] = 0, so total = 0.

So we only need to consider:
- Rows that have at least one target
- Columns that have at least one target

For each such row r and each such column c, we calculate count[r] + count[c] - (1 if there's a target at (r,c) else 0)

We also need to consider that a row might have targets but we could potentially get a better result by choosing a different column.

The maximum possible value will be achieved when we pick a row with high target count and a column with high target count.

So we need:
- The maximum row count
- The maximum column count
- And also consider the possibility where a single position (r,c) contains a target (so we subtract 1)

Let me implement this:

```python
# Read input
H, W, M = map(int, input().split())
targets = []
row_count = [0] * (H + 1)
col_count = [0] * (W + 1)
target_set = set()

for _ in range(M):
    h, w = map(int, input().split())
    targets.append((h, w))
    row_count[h] += 1
    col_count[w] += 1
    target_set.add((h, w))

# Find maximum destruction
max_destroyed = 0

# Try all rows that have targets and all columns that have targets
rows_with_targets = [i for i in range(1, H + 1) if row_count[i] > 0]
cols_with_targets = [j for j in range(1, W + 1) if col_count[j] > 0]

for r in rows_with_targets:
    for c in cols_with_targets:
        # Targets in row r + targets in column c - 1 if there's a target at (r,c)
        destroyed = row_count[r] + col_count[c]
        if (r, c) in target_set:
            destroyed -= 1
        max_destroyed = max(max_destroyed, destroyed)

# Also consider the possibility of placing bomb at position with no target
# But if we place at a position with no target, it's just row_count[r] + col_count[c]
# This is already covered in the above loop since we're considering all rows/cols with targets

print(max_destroyed)
```

Wait, this might be too slow. The number of rows with targets and columns with targets could be up to 3×10⁵ each, so the double loop could be O(M²) which is 9×10¹⁰ operations - too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

## Optimized Approach

The maximum value of row_count[r] + col_count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c)) occurs when we pick the row with maximum row_count and the column with maximum col_count.

But if there's a target at (max_row, max_col), then we need to subtract 1, otherwise we just add them.

So:
1. Find the maximum row count and the maximum column count
2. Check if there's a target at the intersection of the row with max row count and the column with max col count
3. The answer is max_row_count + max_col_count - (1 if target exists at that intersection else 0)

But is this always optimal? 

Consider: what if there's a row with high row_count but not the maximum, and a column with high col_count but not the maximum? Could that give us a better result?

The expression is row_count[r] + col_count[c] - (1 if target exists at (r,c)). 

Since we're adding two positive values and possibly subtracting 1, the maximum occurs when both row_count[r] and col_count[c] are as large as possible. The sum row_count[r] + col_count[c] is maximized when r has high row_count and c has high col_count.

For any two values a and b, a + b is maximized when a and b are as large as possible.

So yes, the maximum of row_count[r] + col_count[c] occurs at the row with maximum row_count and column with maximum col_count.

The only exception is if we subtract 1 when there's a target at (r,c). But if we have two different pairs (r1,c1) and (r2,c2), we might get a better value when the intersection doesn't have a target.

However, since row_count[r] + col_count[c] is maximized at the row with maximum row_count and column with maximum col_count, any other combination will give a smaller sum. Even if we don't have a target at (r,c), the sum is still smaller than the maximum possible sum.

The only way to get a better sum is if we have a lower row_count but higher col_count, or vice versa, but that can't happen because we're maximizing the sum.

Wait, actually: if we have a row with row_count = 100 and column with col_count = 50, the sum is 150.
If we have a row with row_count = 90 and column with col_count = 60, the sum is still 150.
So the maximum sum is achieved by choosing the maximum row_count and maximum col_count.

Therefore, the optimal solution is to pick the row with maximum row_count and the column with maximum col_count, then subtract 1 if there's a target at their intersection.

```python
H, W, M = map(int, input().split())

# Count targets by row and column
row_count = [0] * (H + 1)
col_count = [0] * (W + 1)

# Store targets to check for intersection
targets = []

for _ in range(M):
    h, w = map(int, input().split())
    targets.append((h, w))
    row_count[h] += 1
    col_count[w] += 1

# Find the maximum row count and maximum column count
max_row_count = max(row_count[1:][:H+1]) if H > 0 else 0
max_col_count = max(col_count[1:][:W+1]) if W > 0 else 0

# Check if there's a target at the intersection of the row with max_row_count and column with max_col_count
# We need to find which row has the maximum row_count, and which column has the maximum col_count

    if row_count[i] > row_count[max_row]:
        max_row = i

max_col = 1

    if col_count[j] > col_count[max_col]:
        max_col = j

    if h == max_row and w == max_col:
        has_target_at_intersection = True
        break

if has_target_at_intersection:
    total_destroyed -= 1
```
